        "anchor": "stmt-lem-reflection-quadratic-pairing",
        "exact_quote": "For every nonzero $m\\in R$ and $Z>0$, let $(m)_S=\\mathfrak h\\mathfrak d$ and $\\upsilon=\\upsilon_m$. Then $$\\label{eq:reflection-global-expansion}\n\\begin{split}\n B_m(Z)\n  ={}&\\sum_{\\mathfrak f\\in\\mathcal F(\\mathfrak h)}\n      \\sum_{\\substack{\\mathfrak a'_{\\mathfrak f}\\in\\mathcal A(\\mathfrak f)\\\\\n                      \\mathfrak n'\\\\(\\mathfrak n',\\mathfrak f)=1}}\n      \\sum_{\\substack{u\\in U_6\\\\s_d,s_c\\in B}}\n       \\frac{\\omega_{\\upsilon,\\mathfrak t}}{q_{\\mathfrak n'}}\\\\\n   &{}\\times\\rho_{\\upsilon,\\mathfrak t,s_d}(\\mathfrak d)\n      \\Biggl[\\sum_{\\mathfrak c'\\ \\mathrm{squarefree}}\n       \\frac{\\lambda_{\\upsilon,\\mathfrak t,s_c}(\\mathfrak c')}\n            {q_{\\mathfrak c'}^{1/2}}\n       \\chi_{\\mathfrak d}(c')^3\\\\\n   &{}\\times\\Phi_{\\upsilon,\\kappa_6(\\mathfrak t;s_d,s_c)}\n       \\!\\left(\\frac{Zq_{\\mathfrak a'_{\\mathfrak f}}\n                     q_{\\mathfrak c'}q_{\\mathfrak n'}^3}\n                    {q_{\\mathfrak d}^2q_{\\mathfrak f}^2}\\right)\\Biggr].\n\\end{split}$$ All ideal sums are over integral exterior ideals, and the expansion is absolutely convergent. The coefficients satisfy $$|\\omega_{\\upsilon,\\mathfrak t}|\n     \\le\\beta_{\\mathfrak f}(\\mathfrak a'_{\\mathfrak f}),\\qquad\n |\\rho_{\\upsilon,\\mathfrak t,s_d}|\\le1,\\qquad\n |\\lambda_{\\upsilon,\\mathfrak t,s_c}|\\le1.$$ The row-unit class $\\upsilon$ is fixed; $u$ is the independently summed frequency-unit class.",
        "source_id": "PM-029.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-reflection-quadratic-sieve",
        "exact_quote": "For $C,D\\geq1$ and complex coefficients $z_{\\mathfrak c}$ supported on squarefree exterior ideals with $q_{\\mathfrak c}\\leq C$, one has, for every $\\epsilon>0$, $$\\label{eq:reflection-quadratic-sieve}\n \\sum_{\\substack{\\mathfrak d\\ \\mathrm{squarefree}\\\\q_{\\mathfrak d}\\leq D}}\n   \\left|\\sum_{\\substack{\\mathfrak c\\ \\mathrm{squarefree}\\\\q_{\\mathfrak c}\\leq C}}\n       z_{\\mathfrak c}\\chi_{\\mathfrak d}(c)^3\\right|^2\n \\ll_{F,S,\\epsilon}(C+D)(CD)^\\epsilon\n             \\sum_{\\mathfrak c}|z_{\\mathfrak c}|^2.$$ Arbitrary restrictions on the rows and separate restrictions or unitary factors on the columns are allowed by deleting rows and changing the coefficients.",
        "source_id": "PM-029.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-reflection-quadratic-sieve",
          "exact_quote": "For $C,D\\geq1$ and complex coefficients $z_{\\mathfrak c}$ supported on squarefree exterior ideals with $q_{\\mathfrak c}\\leq C$, one has, for every $\\epsilon>0$, $$\\label{eq:reflection-quadratic-sieve}\n \\sum_{\\substack{\\mathfrak d\\ \\mathrm{squarefree}\\\\q_{\\mathfrak d}\\leq D}}\n   \\left|\\sum_{\\substack{\\mathfrak c\\ \\mathrm{squarefree}\\\\q_{\\mathfrak c}\\leq C}}\n       z_{\\mathfrak c}\\chi_{\\mathfrak d}(c)^3\\right|^2\n \\ll_{F,S,\\epsilon}(C+D)(CD)^\\epsilon\n             \\sum_{\\mathfrak c}|z_{\\mathfrak c}|^2.$$ Arbitrary restrictions on the rows and separate restrictions or unitary factors on the columns are allowed by deleting rows and changing the coefficients.",
          "source_id": "PM-029.1"
        }

      "exact_statement": "For $C,D\\geq1$ and complex coefficients $z_{\\mathfrak c}$ supported on squarefree exterior ideals with $q_{\\mathfrak c}\\leq C$, one has, for every $\\epsilon>0$, $$\\label{eq:reflection-quadratic-sieve}\n \\sum_{\\substack{\\mathfrak d\\ \\mathrm{squarefree}\\\\q_{\\mathfrak d}\\leq D}}\n   \\left|\\sum_{\\substack{\\mathfrak c\\ \\mathrm{squarefree}\\\\q_{\\mathfrak c}\\leq C}}\n       z_{\\mathfrak c}\\chi_{\\mathfrak d}(c)^3\\right|^2\n \\ll_{F,S,\\epsilon}(C+D)(CD)^\\epsilon\n             \\sum_{\\mathfrak c}|z_{\\mathfrak c}|^2.$$ Arbitrary restrictions on the rows and separate restrictions or unitary factors on the columns are allowed by deleting rows and changing the coefficients.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-additive-sieve",
        "exact_quote": "Let $K$ be a fixed compact subset of $F_S$, and let $N,V\\ge2$. Choose balanced generators $s$ for the exterior ideals with $q_{\\mathfrak s}\\le V$. Let $\\mathcal P$ be any subset of the distinct points $$\\left\\{\\frac d s+R:\n       q_{\\mathfrak s}\\le V,\\ d\\in(R/\\mathfrak s)^\\times\\right\\}\n       \\subset F_S/R.$$ For any complex $b_\\alpha$, $$\\sum_{m\\in R\\cap\\ell_NK}\n       \\left|\\sum_{\\alpha\\in\\mathcal P}b_\\alpha\\mathrm{e}(m\\alpha)\\right|^2\n \\ll_{F,S,K}(N+V^2)\\sum_{\\alpha\\in\\mathcal P}|b_\\alpha|^2 .\n\\label{eq:additive-sieve}$$ The dual inequality, with the sums over $m$ and $\\alpha$ interchanged, has the same constant.",
        "source_id": "PM-029.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-additive-sieve",

          "source_id": "PM-029.1"
        }
      ],

        "anchor": "stmt-prop-euler-product",
        "exact_quote": "For every $u\\in\\mathcal U$, the series in [eq:calF-definition](#eq:calF-definition) has the Euler factorization $$\\mathcal F_u(\\xi,w,z)=\n \\frac{\\zeta_F^S(6z)L^S(w,\\chi_\\bullet(u))}\n      {L^S(\\xi,\\eta\\overline{\\chi_\\bullet(u)})}\n             \\mathcal H_u(\\xi,w,z)\n\\label{eq:probe-euler-product}$$ initially in absolute convergence. The correction $\\mathcal H_u$ is holomorphic in open neighborhoods of both real-part regions $$\\begin{array}{lll}\n \\operatorname{Re}\\xi\\ge .998,&\\operatorname{Re}w\\ge .003,&\\operatorname{Re}z\\ge .4,\\\\\n \\operatorname{Re}\\xi\\ge .998,&\\operatorname{Re}w\\ge .95,&\\operatorname{Re}z\\ge .16.\n \\end{array}\n\\label{eq:euler-regions}$$ For every $\\epsilon>0$ it satisfies $$|\\mathcal H_u(\\xi,w,z)|\\ll_{F,S,\\eta,\\epsilon}q_u^\\epsilon$$ there, uniformly in all heights. If $S$ contains every prime of norm at most a sufficiently large fixed constant, then $\\mathcal H_1$ is bounded and nowhere zero on both neighborhoods.",

The correction $\\mathcal H_u$ is holomorphic in open neighborhoods of both real-part regions $$\\begin{array}{lll}\n \\operatorname{Re}\\xi\\ge .998,&\\operatorname{Re}w\\ge .003,&\\operatorname{Re}z\\ge .4,\\\\\n \\operatorname{Re}\\xi\\ge .998,&\\operatorname{Re}w\\ge .95,&\\operatorname{Re}z\\ge .16.\n \\end{array}\n\\label{eq:euler-regions}$$ For every $\\epsilon>0$ it satisfies $$|\\mathcal H_u(\\xi,w,z)|\\ll_{F,S,\\eta,\\epsilon}q_u^\\epsilon$$ there, uniformly in all heights.     {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-conductor-sieve",
        "exact_quote": "Fix $C_0>0$ and a finite set $\\mathscr L_S$ of possible local components at $S$. Let $\\mathscr X(U)$ be any family of distinct primitive finite-order Hecke characters of $F$ whose conductor norms are at most $C_0U$ and whose components at $S$ belong to that set. For $N,U\\ge2$, any complex coefficients $b_{\\mathfrak n}$, and every $\\epsilon>0$, $$\\sum_{\\omega\\in\\mathscr X(U)}\n \\left|\\sum_{q_{\\mathfrak n}\\le N}\n               b_{\\mathfrak n}\\omega(\\mathfrak n)\\right|^2\n \\ll_{F,S,C_0,\\mathscr L_S,\\epsilon}\n       (N+U^2)(NU)^\\epsilon\n       \\sum_{q_{\\mathfrak n}\\le N}|b_{\\mathfrak n}|^2 .\n\\label{eq:conductor-sieve}$$ As usual a primitive character is extended by zero on ideals meeting its conductor.",
        "source_id": "PM-029.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-conductor-sieve",
          "exact_quote": "Fix $C_0>0$ and a finite set $\\mathscr L_S$ of possible local components at $S$. Let $\\mathscr X(U)$ be any family of distinct primitive finite-order Hecke characters of $F$ whose conductor norms are at most $C_0U$ and whose components at $S$ belong to that set. For $N,U\\ge2$, any complex coefficients $b_{\\mathfrak n}$, and every $\\epsilon>0$, $$\\sum_{\\omega\\in\\mathscr X(U)}\n \\left|\\sum_{q_{\\mathfrak n}\\le N}\n               b_{\\mathfrak n}\\omega(\\mathfrak n)\\right|^2\n \\ll_{F,S,C_0,\\mathscr L_S,\\epsilon}\n       (N+U^2)(NU)^\\epsilon\n       \\sum_{q_{\\mathfrak n}\\le N}|b_{\\mathfrak n}|^2 .\n\\label{eq:conductor-sieve}$$ As usual a primitive character is extended by zero on ideals meeting its conductor.",
          "source_id": "PM-029.1"
        }
      ],

        "anchor": "stmt-prop-zero-density",
        "exact_quote": "For the fixed $F,S,\\eta$ there are $\\rho>0$ and $U_0$ with the following property.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-spl-field-bounds",
        "exact_quote": "Fix an integer $a$ with $|a|>1$. For every sufficiently large prime $q$, the degree $n_q=[K_q:\\mathbb{Q}]$ and absolute discriminant $D_q$ satisfy $$n_q=q(q-1),\\qquad\n D_q\\le q^{q(q-2)}(q^q|a|^{q-1})^{q-1},\\qquad\n \\log D_q+n_q\\ll_a n_q\\log(2q).\n \\label{spl:degree-discriminant}$$ Every rational prime $p\\nmid aq$ is unramified in $K_q$, and $$\\zeta_{K_q}(s)\\ne0\n \\qquad(\\operatorname{Re}s>1-\\delta_0,\\ s\\ne1).\n \\label{spl:kummer-zero-free}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-029.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

        "anchor": "stmt-spl-smooth-estimate",
        "exact_quote": "Fix $0<\\delta<1/2$ and a nonnegative function $f\\in C_c^\\infty((0,\\infty))$. Let $K$ be a number field of degree $n$ and absolute discriminant $D$ whose Dedekind zeta function has no zero in $\\operatorname{Re}s>1-\\delta$. For every real $x\\ge2$, $$\\Theta_{K,f}(x)\n :=\\sum_{\\mathfrak p}\\sum_{j\\ge1}\n       (\\log\\operatorname{N}\\mathfrak p)\n       f\\left(\\frac{(\\operatorname{N}\\mathfrak p)^j}{x}\\right)\n \\ll_f x+x^{1-\\delta}(\\log D+n),\n \\label{spl:smooth-prime-ideal-bound}$$ where $\\mathfrak p$ ranges over the nonzero prime ideals of $K$.         "exact_quote": "Fix $\\delta,C,D_*>0$ and $q\\in(0,1)$. Suppose $H_m,H_n\\ge x^\\delta$ and $X=H_mH_n\\asymp x$. Let $F$ satisfy $|F(h)|\\le1$ and $F(ph)=F(h)$ for every prime in the groups defined in [eq:marked-prime-groups](#eq:marked-prime-groups). Let $\\alpha_m$ be supported on an arbitrary interval in $[H_m,2H_m]$, where it has the value $$\\alpha_m=m^{iv}\\left(\\mathbf{1}_{m\\text{ prime}}\n       -\\frac{\\mathbf{1}_{P^-(m)>W}}{V(W)\\log m}\\right),\n \\qquad |v|\\le L^C.$$ Let $\\beta_n$ be supported on $W$-rough integers in $[H_n,2H_n]$, with $|\\beta_n|\\le L^C$. If $K$ is sufficiently large in terms of $\\delta,C,D_*,q$, then $$\\label{eq:marked-type-ii}\n \\left|\\sum_{m,n}\\alpha_m\\beta_n F(mn-1)\\mathcal W(mn-1)\\right|\n       \\ll X L^{-D_*}.$$ The required lower bound on $K$ is independent of the particular $a_i$. The implicit constant and the threshold for $x$ may depend on all the fixed parameters, including the $a_i$.",
        "source_id": "PM-029.1"
      },

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-marked-coefficient-transfer",
        "exact_quote": "Fix $\\delta,C,D_*>0$ and $q\\in(0,1)$. Retain the marked data and the hypotheses on $F,H_m,H_n$ in [thm:marked-type-ii](#thm:marked-type-ii), with fixed comparison constants in $X=H_mH_n\\asymp x$. Let $(\\alpha_m)$ and $(\\beta_n)$ be scalar sequences indexed by positive integers, supported on $W$-rough integers in their respective dyads, with $\\alpha$ supported on an arbitrary interval $I\\subseteq[H_m,2H_m]$, and with $|\\alpha_m|,|\\beta_n|\\le L^C$. Suppose that for every fixed $A_0,B,A>0$, every Dirichlet character $\\chi$ modulo $k\\le L^{A_0}$, and every $|t|\\le2XL^B$, $$\\label{eq:type-ii-long-coefficient-interface}\n \\left|\\frac1{H_m}\\sum_m\\alpha_m\\chi(m)m^{it}\\right|\\ll_A L^{-A},$$ uniformly in the permitted interval, character, and frequency. The constant and threshold in this hypothesis may depend on $A_0,B,A$, the fixed setup, and all further fixed parameters defining the coefficients; these parameters are chosen before $x$ grows.*\n\n*Then [eq:marked-type-ii](#eq:marked-type-ii) holds when $K$ is sufficiently large in terms of the fixed data, independently of the particular band exponents $a_i$. Its implicit constant and threshold may depend on all the fixed data, including the $a_i$.",

        "anchor": "stmt-prop-rough-type-ii",
        "exact_quote": "Retain the marked data and all the hypotheses on $F,\\beta,H_m,H_n$ of [thm:marked-type-ii](#thm:marked-type-ii). Fix real numbers $0<\\gamma<w_-<w_+<1$, and suppose $$[\\log H_m/L,\\log(2H_m)/L]\\subseteq[w_-,w_+].$$ On an arbitrary interval $I\\subseteq[H_m,2H_m]$, set $$\\label{eq:rough-type-ii-coefficient}\n \\alpha_m=m^{iv}\\left(\n    \\mathbf{1}_{P^-(m)>x^\\gamma}\n    -\\frac{D_\\gamma(\\log m/L)}{L V(W)}\\mathbf{1}_{P^-(m)>W}\n                         \\right),\\qquad |v|\\le L^C,$$ and set $\\alpha_m=0$ outside $I$. Then [eq:marked-type-ii](#eq:marked-type-ii) holds for every fixed $D_*>0$ when $K$ is sufficiently large in terms of the fixed data. Its required lower bound is independent of the particular band exponents $a_i$; the threshold and implicit constant may depend on all the fixed data. Then [eq:marked-type-ii](#eq:marked-type-ii) holds for every fixed $D_*>0$ when $K$ is sufficiently large in terms of the fixed data.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-block",
        "exact_quote": "Let $\\mathcal A$ be a finite set with nonnegative weights, and let $\\mathcal P$ be a set of primes at most $z$, where $z\\ge2$. For each $p\\in\\mathcal P$, specify a bad condition on $\\mathcal A$. Suppose that, for every squarefree product $d$ of primes in $\\mathcal P$, the weight of the objects satisfying all conditions at $p\\mid d$ is $$A(d)=X'g(d)+E(d),$$ including $d=1$, where $X'\\ge0$, $g(1)=1$, and $g$ is multiplicative. Assume that, for fixed constants $\\eta_0>0$ and $C_0<\\infty$, $$\\label{eq:block-hypotheses}\n 0\\le g(p)\\le1-\\eta_0,\n \\qquad\n \\sum_{\\substack{v<p\\le v^2\\\\p\\in\\mathcal P}}g(p)\\le C_0\n \\quad(v>1).$$ For every sufficiently large even integer $H$, the weight $S$ of objects avoiding all the bad conditions satisfies $$\\label{eq:block-sieve}\n S=X'\\prod_{p\\in\\mathcal P}(1-g(p))\n       \\bigl(1+O(\\exp(-H))\\bigr)\n   +O\\left(\\sum_{\\substack{d\\le z^{4H+2}\\\\d\\mid\\prod_{p\\in\\mathcal P}p}}\n                  |E(d)|\\right).$$ The constants and the lower bound on $H$ depend only on $\\eta_0,C_0$. In particular, the estimate is uniform when $H$ grows. For $H=2$ there is the upper bound $$\\label{eq:block-sieve-upper}\n S\\ll_{\\eta_0,C_0}X'\\prod_{p\\in\\mathcal P}(1-g(p))\n       +\\sum_{\\substack{d\\le z^{10}\\\\d\\mid\\prod_{p\\in\\mathcal P}p}}\n                    |E(d)|.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-029.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-block",

          "source_id": "PM-029.1"

        "anchor": "stmt-con-harmonic",
        "exact_quote": "For each group put $$E_i(t)=\\prod_{p\\in\\mathcal P_i}\\left(1+\\frac{t}{p-1}\\right).$$ The harmonic mass has the exact Euler-product expression $$\\label{con:harmonic-identity}\n \\begin{aligned}\n J_0&=\\prod_{i=1}^K\\frac{E_i'(1/2)}{V_i}\\\\\n    &=\\prod_{i=1}^K\\left\\{\n       \\frac{E_i(1/2)}{V_i}\n       \\sum_{p\\in\\mathcal P_i}\\frac1{p-1+1/2}\\right\\}.\n \\end{aligned}$$ For every fixed $\\varepsilon>0$, $$\\label{con:harmonic-bounds}\n 1\\le J_0\\ll_K1,\\qquad\n \\sum_{r>x^\\varepsilon}\\frac{\\mathcal W(r)}r\n       \\ll_K\\exp(-\\varepsilon L^{1-a_K}).$$ Consequently the part of $X_0$ with $r>x^\\varepsilon$ is $O_K(x\\exp(-\\varepsilon L^{1-a_K}))$.",
        "source_id": "PM-029.1"

        "exact_quote": "Let $\\mathcal B=[M_1,2M_1)\\times[M_2,2M_2)$ be any dyadic box such that $$\\label{con:rough-box-range}\n x/4\\le M_1M_2\\le2x,\\qquad\n x^{0.46}/2\\le M_i\\le2x^{0.54}\\quad(i=1,2).$$ Then $$\\label{con:rough-pair-bound}\n \\sum_{\\substack{(m,n)\\in\\mathcal B\\\\P^-(m)>W,\\ P^-(n)>W}}w(mn)\n       \\le C_1X_0V(W)^2,$$ where $C_1$ depends at most on $M,\\Psi$, and is independent of $K,\\kappa,b,\\varepsilon$.",

      "exact_statement": "Let $\\mathcal B=[M_1,2M_1)\\times[M_2,2M_2)$ be any dyadic box such that $$\\label{con:rough-box-range}\n x/4\\le M_1M_2\\le2x,\\qquad\n x^{0.46}/2\\le M_i\\le2x^{0.54}\\quad(i=1,2).$$ Then $$\\label{con:rough-pair-bound}\n \\sum_{\\substack{(m,n)\\in\\mathcal B\\\\P^-(m)>W,\\ P^-(n)>W}}w(mn)\n       \\le C_1X_0V(W)^2,$$ where $C_1$ depends at most on $M,\\Psi$, and is independent of $K,\\kappa,b,\\varepsilon$.",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **multiplicative order** ([source](#sec:introduction)): The multiplicative order $\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_p(a)$ is the least positive integer $k$ such that $a^k\equiv1\pmod p$.
- **primitive root** ([source](#sec:introduction)): The residue class of $a$ is a primitive root modulo $p$ if $\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_p(a)=p-1$.
- **admissible integer** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Call $a$ *admissible* if $a\ne-1$ and $a$ is not the square of an integer.
- **finite-order Hecke character** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A finite-order Hecke character of a number field $F$ means a continuous finite-image character of the idele class group $F^\times\backslash\mathbb{A}_F^\times$.
- **exterior ideal** ([source](#sec:arithmetic)): An *exterior ideal* is a nonzero integral ideal of $R$, equivalently an ideal supported at primes outside $S$.

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-splitting-test** [claim](#stmt-alg-splitting-test) / [proof](#proof-stmt-alg-splitting-test): Frobenius action on roots of T^q-a and on q-th roots of unity -> complete splitting iff two congruences
- **step-quadratic-progression** [claim](#stmt-alg-quadratic-progression) / [proof](#proof-stmt-alg-quadratic-progression): Quadratic reciprocity and Chinese remainder theorem over a finite table -> progression with nonresidue condition
- **step-field-bounds** [claim](#stmt-spl-field-bounds) / [proof](#proof-stmt-spl-field-bounds): Discriminant tower formula and abelian Artin factorization -> degree, discriminant and zero-free width for the Kummer field
- **step-smooth-estimate** [claim](#stmt-spl-smooth-estimate) / [proof](#proof-stmt-spl-smooth-estimate): Mellin inversion and contour shift in the explicit formula -> smoothed prime-ideal bound
- **step-uniform-splitting** [claim](#stmt-prop-splitting) / [proof](#proof-stmt-spl-smooth-estimate-2): Apply the smooth estimate to the Kummer field with a test function -> uniform splitting bound
- **step-reflected-moment** [claim](#stmt-prop-reflection) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-reflection-quadratic-sieve-2): Theta reflection expansion, quadratic large sieve, dyadic summation -> reflected second moment bound
- **step-probe-low** [claim](#stmt-prop-probe-low) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-probe-low): Additive large sieve and Cauchy-Schwarz against the reflected moment -> upper bound for the probe
- **step-euler-product** [claim](#stmt-prop-euler-product) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-euler-product): Local Gauss sum evaluation and Euler factorization -> factorization with holomorphic correction
- **step-zero-density** [claim](#stmt-prop-zero-density) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-zero-density): Zero detector with conductor large sieve and Borel-Caratheodory -> exceptional set and reciprocal bound
- **step-hecke-zero-free** [claim](#stmt-thm-hecke-zero-free) / [proof](#pm-h-27): Compare Poisson evaluation with reflection bound and identify the Mellin transform -> zero-free half-plane
- **step-prime-reservoir** [claim](#stmt-prop-prime-reservoir) / [proof](#proof-stmt-con-rough-pairs-2): Weighted sieve, marked Type II subtraction by least prime factor, upper bound for close factors -> many primes with controlled predecessor
- **step-main** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-alg-quadratic-progression-2): Index divisor reduction with exceptional large prime and Kummer splitting -> lower bound for primitive-root primes

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-splitting-test**: For a nonzero integer a and primes p, q with p not dividing a q, p splits completely in the Kummer field K_q exactly when p is congruent to 1 modulo q and a raised to (p-1)/q is congruent to 1 modulo p. A prime q dividing the index (p-1)/ord_p(a) therefore witnesses exactly this congruence, so a is a primitive root modulo p exactly when no prime divisor q of p-1 passes the test.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-prime-divides-base**: Apply the congruence test when the prime p divides the base a or the prime q. -> The equivalence with complete splitting is no longer asserted, and the power of a is zero or the discriminant is divisible by p, so the test result carries no splitting meaning.
- **anti-nonprime-modulus**: Run the test for a composite modulus in place of the prime p, or for a non-prime q, and read the output as primitive-root status. -> The Frobenius argument behind the equivalence needs p and q prime, so the verdict is not justified for composite values.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `algorithmic`. The splitting test of the lemma is a finite congruence check, and iterating it over the prime divisors of p-1 gives a finite procedure deciding whether a is a primitive root modulo a given prime p.

```python
# INPUTS: one line with two integers a and p separated by a space
# PRECONDITIONS: p is prime and p does not divide a
# OUTPUTS: prints 1 if a is a primitive root modulo p, otherwise 0
# TIME: trial division up to sqrt(p) plus one modular power per prime divisor of p-1
# SPACE: O(number of distinct prime divisors of p-1)
# ARITHMETIC: exact Python integers, modular exponentiation
# FAILURE: malformed input raises an exception and exits nonzero
import sys
a, p = map(int, sys.stdin.read().split())
n=p-1
qs = []
m = n
d = 2
while d * d <= m:
    if m % d == 0:
        qs.append(d)
        while m % d == 0:
            m //= d
    d += 1
if m>1: qs.append(m)
ok = (a % p != 0)
for q in qs:
    if pow(a, n//q, p) == 1:
        ok = False
print(1 if ok else 0)
```

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper proves an asymptotic lower bound on counts of primes. It does not specify a library, API or program interface, and the constants and thresholds depend on the base, so there is nothing to ship as software. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the paper concerns simulation, procedural generation or game mechanics. The setting is analytic number theory over cyclotomic fields. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No tool, checker or generator is described. The sieve and theta constructions are proof devices whose parameters are fixed only to make estimates close, not to be run. |
| algorithms | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The elementary reduction in the paper is finite and checkable. For a prime p not dividing a, a prime q dividing the index of a modulo p is detected by the complete-splitting test of the splitting-test lemma, and the progression lemma gives a computable residue class forcing a to be a quadratic nonresidue. These two pieces form a verification routine for a candidate prime. The prime-construction and zero-free parts are non-constructive for implementation, so only the verification layer is adaptable. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper introduces no data structure. Its objects are ideals, characters and analytic weights used only inside proofs. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No arithmetic kernel, circuit or accelerator is analysed. The Poisson summation and Mellin arguments have no hardware mapping in the paper. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | A fixed small base generating the full group is the natural cryptographic motivation, but the theorem gives only a lower bound for large enough x with base-dependent, non-explicit constants. It offers no certificate for a specific prime and no security statement, and the constructed primes have a predecessor with intermediate prime divisors. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no learning problem, loss or model in the paper. The only statistics are prime counts and character sums. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No objective or constraint system is optimised. Parameter choices in the sieve are made to satisfy inequalities, not to minimise a cost. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper has no notion of nodes, messages or consensus. Complete splitting of a prime in a Kummer field is an algebraic condition, not a protocol event. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No further engineering domain emerges. The main value is theoretical: an unconditional infinitude result for each admissible base together with a uniform Hecke zero-free half-plane. |

          "exact_quote": "<span id=\"prop:controlled\" label=\"prop:controlled\"></span>Let $M$ be a fixed positive multiple of $8$, let $c\\in\\{2,4\\}$, and let $u$ be an integer satisfying $$(u,M)=1,\\qquad c\\mid u-1,\\qquad\n \\left(\\frac{u-1}{c},\\frac Mc\\right)=1.\n \\label{alg:progression-conditions}$$ For every sufficiently large real $x$, there are $\\gg_{M,c,u}x/L^2$ distinct primes $p\\in(x,2x)$ satisfying $$p\\equiv u\\pmod M,\\qquad p-1=crQ,\n \\qquad Q>x^{0.9}\\ \\text{prime},\n \\label{eq:controlled-predecessor}$$ where $r$ is a positive integer all of whose prime divisors lie in $$\\bigl(\\exp(L^{0.1}),\\exp(L^{0.3})\\bigr).$$",
          "source_object": "stmt-prop-prime-reservoir"
        }
      ],
      "space_complexity": null,
      "strength": "none",
      "time_complexity": null,

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-admissible-base** (hypothesis): The main lower bound is proved only for admissible bases, meaning the base is not minus one and is not the square of an integer. For other bases primitive-root behavior at infinitely many primes is impossible by the stated obstructions, so nothing is claimed. Blocked use: ['Applying the bound to the base minus one or to a perfect square to obtain primitive roots modulo large primes.', 'Reading the theorem as a statement about every integer base.']
- **limit-base-dependent-constants** (nonconstructive): The positive constant and the threshold in the main bound depend on the base, and the paper states no explicit values for them. Blocked use: ['Quoting a concrete size beyond which a given prime is guaranteed to exist for a given base.', 'Using the bound as a finite-range density guarantee or as a certificate for one specific prime.']
- **limit-asymptotic-regime** (hypothesis): The prime reservoir is asserted only for all sufficiently large x, with a count whose implied constant depends on the fixed progression data. The paper gives no explicit threshold for x. Blocked use: ['Invoking the reservoir at a concrete small scale without separate verification.', 'Treating the count as uniform in the progression data.']
- **limit-progression-data** (hypothesis): The reservoir needs a fixed modulus that is a multiple of eight, a cofactor in the stated two-element set, and a residue class satisfying the stated coprimality conditions. Without these local conditions the construction is not available. Blocked use: ['Producing primes with a controlled predecessor in an arbitrary residue class or modulus.', 'Dropping the coprimality condition on the residue class.']
- **limit-hecke-field-class** (formal_scope): The uniform zero-free half-plane is proved for cyclotomic number fields containing the twelfth roots of unity and for finite-order Hecke characters only. Blocked use: ['Applying the stated width to arbitrary number fields that are not cyclotomic or lack the twelfth roots of unity.', 'Applying it to infinite-order Hecke characters or to general automorphic L-functions.']
- **limit-principal-pole** (edge_case): For the principal character the pole at one is permitted, so the statement concerns zeros of the meromorphic function and does not say the function is holomorphic. Blocked use: ['Inferring holomorphy of the L-function in the half-plane for the principal character.', 'Dropping the pole when the principal character is among the characters considered.']
- **limit-splitting-range-of-q** (hypothesis): The uniform splitting bound covers only primes q that are sufficiently large in terms of the base and at most the stated subexponential bound in log x. Outside that range no uniform bound is asserted. Blocked use: ['Summing the bound over all primes q without the upper cutoff.', 'Applying it to small q without the dependence on the base.']
- **limit-splitting-base-size** (hypothesis): The uniform splitting bound is stated for a base of absolute value greater than one. The proof of the reduction also needs that no power of the base equals one modulo the prime. Blocked use: ['Using the bound for the bases zero, one or minus one.', 'Using the divisor-product argument when some power of the base minus one vanishes.']
- **limit-splitting-test-primes** (hypothesis): The splitting criterion by two congruences is valid only for primes p and q with p not dividing the base times q. For other p the equivalence with complete splitting is not asserted. Blocked use: ['Running the congruence test when p divides the base or q.', 'Using the test with a composite modulus or a non-prime q and reading it as splitting information.']
- **limit-odd-powers-remark** (edge_case): The remark that odd perfect powers need no separate exclusion is made for a fixed odd exponent and concerns the constructed primes. It is not an independent theorem about all exponents or all primes. Blocked use: ['Using the remark with an exponent that grows with x.', 'Reading it as covering primes outside the constructed reservoir.']
- **limit-explicit-threshold-absent** (unknown): The paper does not give numerical values for the base-dependent constant or for the threshold in the main bound, nor an effective procedure that outputs them. Blocked use: ['Using any specific numerical constant or threshold as if taken from the paper.']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove the infinitude assertion in Artin's primitive root conjecture: for every integer <i>a</i> that is neither −1 nor a square, there are at least $`c_a x/(\log x)^2`$ primes in $`(x,2x)`$ with primitive root <i>a</i>, for some $`c_a\gt 0`$ and every sufficiently large <i>x</i>.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Let $a$ be an integer and let $p$ be a prime not dividing $a$. The multiplicative order $\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_p(a)$ is the least positive integer $k$ such that $a^k\equiv1\pmod p$. The residue class of $a$ is a primitive root modulo $p$ if $\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_p(a)=p-1$. Call $a$ *admissible* if $a\ne-1$ and $a$ is not the square of an integer. These are necessary conditions for primitive-root behavior at infinitely many primes: the order of $-1$ is at most $2$, and a nonzero square modulo an odd prime has order dividing $(p-1)/2$.

Artin’s primitive root conjecture, formulated in 1927, asserts that every admissible integer is a primitive root modulo infinitely many primes; its quantitative form predicts a positive-density asymptotic <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[ArtinHasse08](#ref-ArtinHasse08), Section 17.2]. We prove the following lower bound.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *<span id="thm:primitive-root" label="thm:primitive-root"></span>For every admissible integer $a$, there are constants $c_a>0$ and $x_a\ge2$ such that, for every real $x\ge x_a$, $$\#\{p\text{ prime}:x<p<2x,\ p\nmid a,\ \mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_p(a)=p-1\}
       \ge c_a\frac{x}{(\log x)^2}.$$*

</div>

The predicted counting scale is $x/\log x$; the theorem gives $x/(\log x)^2$ in every sufficiently large dyadic interval. In particular, it proves the infinitude assertion for each fixed admissible integer, including the prescribed base $2$. The constants and the threshold may depend on the base.

The analytic part of the proof establishes a separate theorem. A finite-order Hecke character of a number field $F$ means a continuous finite-image character of the idele class group $F^\times\backslash\mathbb{A}_F^\times$. Write $\mu_m$ for the group of $m$th roots of unity.

<a id="stmt-thm-hecke-zero-free"></a>
<div id="thm:hecke-zero-free" class="theorem">

**Theorem 2**. *Let $F$ be a cyclotomic number field containing $\mu_{12}$. For every finite-order Hecke character $\eta$ of $F$, the meromorphic continuation of $L_F(s,\eta)$ has no zeros in $$\operatorname{Re}s>1-10^{-6}.$$*

</div>

The principal character is included, with its pole at $s=1$ permitted. The width is common to all fields and characters in the statement, with no conductor or height cutoff. In the proof, $F$ and $\eta$ are fixed before the auxiliary data are chosen. Constants and the points from which estimates hold may depend on them; the numerical saving in the final Mellin argument does not. This distinction is what allows a uniform splitting estimate for a growing family of fields.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Historical context and the two obstructions

Hooley proved Artin’s predicted asymptotic under the Riemann hypothesis for the Dedekind zeta functions of the Kummer fields $\mathbb{Q}(\mu_n,a^{1/n})$, with $n$ squarefree <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Hooley67](#ref-Hooley67)]. These fields encode the obstructions to maximal order. For a prime $q$ and $p\nmid aq$, the conditions $$p\equiv1\pmod q,\qquad a^{(p-1)/q}\equiv1\pmod p$$ say that $p$ splits completely in $\mathbb{Q}(\mu_q,a^{1/q})$. They hold precisely when $q$ divides the index $(p-1)/\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_p(a)$. Hooley’s method counts and sums such splitting conditions. Here a restricted factorization of $p-1$ confines the possible index divisors to ranges where they can all be removed.

Classical unconditional results allow a finite choice of bases. Gupta and Murty constructed a set of thirteen integers from three distinct primes, at least one of which is a primitive root modulo infinitely many primes <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[GuptaMurty84](#ref-GuptaMurty84)]. Heath-Brown proved that at most two prime bases fail the infinitude assertion, so at least one of $2,3,5$ satisfies it <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[HeathBrown86](#ref-HeathBrown86)]. Averaging over the base gives another approach. Goldfeld and Stephens established early average theorems <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Goldfeld68](#ref-Goldfeld68), [Stephens69](#ref-Stephens69)]; Klurman, Shparlinski, and Teräväinen obtained the expected asymptotic for almost all integers in a range as short as $1\le a\le\exp((\log\log x)^2)$ <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[KlurmanShparlinskiTeravainen25](#ref-KlurmanShparlinskiTeravainen25)]. The exceptional set may depend on $x$, so these average results do not by themselves settle a prescribed base.

Two difficulties must be addressed for a fixed base. One is analytic: the splitting fields vary with the possible index divisor. A standard simultaneous zero-free region for Hecke characters associated with an abelian extension has logarithmic width depending on the field, conductors, and height, and permits at most one simple real exceptional zero <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[ThornerZaman19](#ref-ThornerZaman19), Theorem 3.1]. The common width in Theorem <a href="#thm:hecke-zero-free" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:hecke-zero-free">2</a> instead gives a common power saving. The other difficulty is the prime construction: we need many primes for which the possible index divisors fall in the range controlled by that saving. We now describe the two parts of the proof and the points where they meet.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The Hecke zero-free argument

Fix a cyclotomic field $F$ containing $\mu_{12}$ and a finite-order Hecke character $\eta$. The analytic construction uses cubic theta coefficients under sextic Kummer twists. Patterson’s cubic theta series over $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-3})$ is the model for the Gauss coefficients and their transformations <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Patterson77](#ref-Patterson77), [Patterson77II](#ref-Patterson77II)]; the comparison formulas are recorded by Dunn and Radziwiłł . The companion manuscript *The Quasi-Riemann Hypothesis* develops a coefficient reflection and an additive probe over that field while retaining unrestricted cube factors <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[QuasiRH](#ref-QuasiRH), Part I, Sections 5–7]. Its zero-free theorem treats Dirichlet $L$-functions and finite-order Hecke $L$-functions over $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-3})$. We construct the required mechanisms over each fixed $F$, using the cubic theta representation of Kazhdan and Patterson and a normalized global Whittaker factorization consistent with their correction <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), [KP85Erratum](#ref-KP85Erratum)].

Two local calculations govern the comparison. We choose generators for which sextic reciprocity has no supplementary factor. In a reflected interaction between a prime occurring to valuation one in the varying twist and a varying squarefree coefficient prime, the sextic exponents are $1$ and $-4$. Their sum is $3$ modulo $6$, so the interaction is quadratic. The quadratic large sieve of Heath-Brown, in its number-field form due to Goldmakher and Louvel, then controls the reflected moment <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[HeathBrown95](#ref-HeathBrown95), [GoldmakherLouvel13](#ref-GoldmakherLouvel13)].

On the Poisson side, a relative-norm formula identifies the cube of the cubic Gauss phase with the negative of a unitary Hecke-character value. A finite-order correction gives the target character $\eta$. At a prime ideal $\mathfrak p$ outside a fixed excluded set, the first squarefree theta term at the principal frequency is $-\eta(\mathfrak p)(\mathrm{N}\mathfrak p)^{-\xi}$, where $\xi$ is the Mellin variable for the coefficient norm. Together with the constant term, it supplies the local factor of the reciprocal target $L$-function. The remaining local terms give a holomorphic, nonvanishing correction in the region used at the end of the proof.

Reflection and Poisson summation thus estimate the same additive average in two ways. A conductor large sieve and zero detection control the nonprincipal frequencies. The resulting power saving for the principal inverse Mellin integral continues its Mellin transform across the line $\operatorname{Re}s=1$ and excludes zeros of the target function. The use of ideal norms keeps the numerical exponents in this argument independent of the degree of $F$. Sections <a href="#sec:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:arithmetic">3</a>–<a href="#sec:zero-free" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:zero-free">8</a> carry out this proof; Appendix <a href="#app:whittaker" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:whittaker">13</a> provides the theta normalization.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Controlled predecessors in a fixed progression

The construction produces many primes of the form $$p=crQ+1,\qquad c\in\{2,4\},\qquad Q>x^{0.9}\text{ prime},$$ where every prime divisor of $r$ lies between $\exp((\log x)^{0.1})$ and $\exp((\log x)^{0.3})$. We also prescribe a residue class on which the Legendre symbol $(a/p)$ equals $-1$. This excludes $2$ as an index divisor. A nontrivial remaining index has a prime divisor in $rQ$: the factor $Q$ leads to a small elementary exceptional set, and the factors of $r$ fall within the uniform splitting estimate.

The construction starts with weighted integers $d=crQ+1$ of this shape. A sieve removes small prime factors of $d$. For a remaining composite $d=mn$, with $n$ its least prime factor, a marked bilinear estimate replaces the restriction on the cofactor $m$ by an explicit density and a weaker condition on its small prime factors. Buchstab’s least-prime-factor identity leaves a positive margin between the initial sifted mass and the mass removed in this comparison. A separate upper bound makes the contribution from two prime factors close to $\sqrt x$ smaller than that margin.

The marked prime estimate is an input from *The Poisson–Dirichlet law for the prime factors of $p-1$ <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[PoissonDirichlet](#ref-PoissonDirichlet), Theorem 3.1]* . We extract its scalar coefficient criterion and verify the full character and frequency estimates for the rough-factor comparison. The weights require $d-1$ to have a prime divisor in each of several prescribed ranges; these choices are the marks. A bounded multiplier in the bilinear estimate may depend on the quotient left after every power of these primes has been removed. A congruence condition on $d=mn$ need not have this invariance. We therefore expand $mn\equiv u\pmod M$ in Dirichlet characters and separate it into factors $\lambda(m)\lambda(n)$ in the two scalar coefficient sequences. Fixed twists preserve the required long character-sum bound.

The resulting fixed-progression construction is stated separately in Proposition <a href="#prop:controlled" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:controlled">[prop:controlled]</a>. It applies to any fixed data $M,c,u$ satisfying its local coprimality conditions, independently of the primitive-root application. Its proof uses Bombieri–Vinogradov distribution, a Brun–Hooley block sieve, and the continuous rough-number density <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Bombieri65](#ref-Bombieri65), [Vinogradov65](#ref-Vinogradov65), [FordHalberstam2000](#ref-FordHalberstam2000), [Buchstab1937](#ref-Buchstab1937)]. The precise elementary sieve and density forms are adapted from <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[BlockPaper](#ref-BlockPaper), Lemmas 2.9 and 7.4], with proofs included here.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Organization and notation

Section <a href="#sec:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:reduction">2</a> states the controlled-predecessor and splitting estimates and combines them to prove Theorem <a href="#thm:primitive-root" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:primitive-root">[thm:primitive-root]</a>. Sections <a href="#sec:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:arithmetic">3</a>–<a href="#sec:zero-free" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:zero-free">8</a> prove Theorem <a href="#thm:hecke-zero-free" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:hecke-zero-free">2</a> for a fixed arbitrary $F$ and $\eta$, independently of the integer base. The analytic scale parameters in those sections are local to that proof. Section <a href="#sec:splitting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:splitting">9</a> returns to the fixed integer $a$ and derives the uniform splitting bound. Section <a href="#sec:type-ii" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:type-ii">10</a> proves the marked coefficient and rough-factor estimates; Section <a href="#sec:sieve" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:sieve">11</a> supplies the elementary sieve facts; Section <a href="#sec:prime-construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:prime-construction">12</a> proves the fixed-progression construction. Appendix <a href="#app:whittaker" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:whittaker">13</a> verifies the selected Kazhdan–Patterson specialization and Whittaker normalization.

Throughout, $\log$ is the natural logarithm, and $L=\log x$ in the prime-counting arguments. The symbols $\ll$ and $O$ have their usual meaning; subscripts record permitted dependence when it matters. Auxiliary parameters are fixed before the relevant asymptotic variable tends to infinity. Uniformity in those parameters is stated explicitly. The general representation-theoretic, class-field, Hecke-continuation, and classical sieve theorems are cited at their points of use. The marked Type II argument also uses the companion papers cited in the bibliography.

<a id="sec:reduction"></a>
### Reduction to controlled predecessors and complete splitting

For a prime $p\nmid a$, failure of $a$ to generate $\mathbb F_p^\times$ means that some prime divides the index $$i_p(a)=\frac{p-1}{\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_p(a)}.$$ We construct primes for which the possible divisors of this index belong to two ranges. A large divisor forces $p$ to divide one of a short list of integers $a^j-1$; the remaining divisors are controlled by complete splitting in Kummer fields. This is the index-divisor reduction underlying Hooley’s work <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Hooley67](#ref-Hooley67), Sections 4–7].

The first proposition supplies the primes, with a fixed congruence that will force quadratic nonresiduosity.

<a id="stmt-prop-prime-reservoir"></a>
<div id="prop:prime-reservoir" class="proposition">

<a id="alg:progression-conditions"></a>
**Proposition 3** (Primes with controlled predecessors). *<span id="prop:controlled" label="prop:controlled"></span>Let $M$ be a fixed positive multiple of $8$, let $c\in\{2,4\}$, and let $u$ be an integer satisfying $$(u,M)=1,\qquad c\mid u-1,\qquad
 \left(\frac{u-1}{c},\frac Mc\right)=1.
 \label{alg:progression-conditions}$$ For every sufficiently large real $x$, there are $\gg_{M,c,u}x/L^2$ distinct primes $p\in(x,2x)$ satisfying <a id="eq:controlled-predecessor"></a>$$p\equiv u\pmod M,\qquad p-1=crQ,
 \qquad Q>x^{0.9}\ \text{prime},
 \label{eq:controlled-predecessor}$$ where $r$ is a positive integer all of whose prime divisors lie in $$\bigl(\exp(L^{0.1}),\exp(L^{0.3})\bigr).$$*

</div>

We prove Proposition <a href="#prop:controlled" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:controlled">[prop:controlled]</a> in Section <a href="#sec:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:construction">[sec:construction]</a>. For the second input, write $\mu_m$ for the group of $m$th roots of unity. For a nonzero integer $a$ and a prime $q$, put <a id="alg:kummer-fields"></a>$$E_q=\mathbb{Q}(\mu_q),\qquad K_q=\mathbb{Q}(\mu_q,a^{1/q}).
 \label{alg:kummer-fields}$$ The choice of the root $a^{1/q}$ does not affect $K_q$.

<a id="stmt-prop-splitting"></a>
<div id="prop:splitting" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:uniform-splitting"></a>
**Proposition 4** (Uniform bound for complete splitting). *Fix an integer $a$ with $|a|>1$, and put $\delta_0=10^{-6}$. For all sufficiently large real $x$ and all primes $q$ sufficiently large in terms of $a$, with $q\le\exp(L^{0.3})$, one has $$\#\{p\text{ prime}:x<p\le2x,\ p\text{ splits completely in }K_q\}
 \ll_a \frac{x}{q(q-1)L}+x^{1-\delta_0}.
 \label{eq:uniform-splitting}$$ The implied constant and the threshold for $x$ are independent of $q$. For $a=2$, every prime $q\ge5$ is allowed, with absolute constants and an absolute threshold for $x$.*

</div>

Section <a href="#sec:splitting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:splitting">9</a> proves this proposition from the Hecke zero-free theorem, Theorem <a href="#thm:hecke-zero-free" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:hecke-zero-free">2</a>. To apply it, we first record the elementary interpretation of complete splitting.

<a id="stmt-alg-splitting-test"></a>
<div id="alg:splitting-test" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:kummer-frobenius-test"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Splitting test). *Let $a\ne0$ be an integer, and let $p,q$ be primes with $p\nmid aq$. Then $p$ splits completely in $K_q$ if and only if $$p\equiv1\pmod q,\qquad a^{(p-1)/q}\equiv1\pmod p.
 \label{eq:kummer-frobenius-test}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-alg-splitting-test"><summary>Proof of stmt-alg-splitting-test</summary>

*Proof.* The field $K_q$ is the splitting field of $T^q-a$. Fix a prime $\mathfrak P$ of $K_q$ above $p$. The roots are integral and have distinct reductions modulo $\mathfrak P$, since $p$ does not divide the polynomial discriminant $\pm q^q a^{q-1}$. Every inertia element permutes the roots and fixes their reductions, so it fixes every root. Thus $p$ is unramified. Let $\sigma$ be its arithmetic Frobenius at $\mathfrak P$. On $\mu_q$, this automorphism acts by $\zeta\mapsto\zeta^p$, so it fixes $E_q$ exactly when $p\equiv1\pmod q$. Under this condition, with $\alpha=a^{1/q}$, we have $$\frac{\sigma(\alpha)}{\alpha}\in\mu_q,
 \qquad
 \frac{\sigma(\alpha)}{\alpha}
 \equiv\alpha^{p-1}=a^{(p-1)/q}\pmod{\mathfrak P}.$$ The division is valid because $p\nmid a$. Reduction on $\mu_q$ is injective at $p\ne q$, so the second congruence in <a href="#eq:kummer-frobenius-test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kummer-frobenius-test">[eq:kummer-frobenius-test]</a> is equivalent to $\sigma(\alpha)=\alpha$. Together the two conditions say that $\sigma$ fixes the generators of $K_q$. Since $K_q/\mathbb{Q}$ is Galois, this is precisely complete splitting. ◻

</details>

We now choose a progression of quadratic nonresidues satisfying all the hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#prop:controlled" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:controlled">[prop:controlled]</a>.

<a id="stmt-alg-quadratic-progression"></a>
<div id="alg:quadratic-progression" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (A progression of quadratic nonresidues). *Let $a\in\mathbb{Z}$ be neither $-1$ nor an integer square, and set $$M=8\prod_{\substack{\ell\mid a\\\ell\text{ odd prime}}}\ell.$$ There are $c\in\{2,4\}$ and an integer $u$ satisfying <a href="#alg:progression-conditions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:progression-conditions">[alg:progression-conditions]</a> such that every prime $p\equiv u\pmod M$ satisfies $p\nmid a$ and $(a/p)=-1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-alg-quadratic-progression"><summary>Proof of stmt-alg-quadratic-progression</summary>

*Proof.* Write $a=d_0t^2$, where $t\ge1$ is an integer and $d_0\ne1$ is signed squarefree. We choose $u$ to be a unit modulo $M$, with $u\not\equiv1\pmod\ell$ for every odd prime $\ell\mid M$, and $u\bmod8\in\{3,5,7\}$. These restrictions already imply $p\nmid a$.

Suppose first that an odd prime $j\ge5$ divides $d_0$. Fix any of the allowed residues modulo $8$ and at the odd primes other than $j$. Quadratic reciprocity expresses $(d_0/p)$ as a fixed sign times $(p/j)$. Among the nonzero residues modulo $j$ other than $1$, there are $(j-3)/2\ge1$ squares and $(j-1)/2$ nonsquares. We can therefore choose $u\bmod j$ so that $(d_0/p)=-1$.

If no such $j$ exists, the seven possibilities for $d_0$ are treated by the following table. Whenever $3\mid M$, also take $u\equiv2\pmod3$.

<div class="center">

|   $d_0$   | $-1$ | $2$ | $-2$ | $3$ | $-3$ | $6$ | $-6$ |
|:---------:|-----:|----:|-----:|----:|-----:|----:|-----:|
| $u\bmod8$ |  $3$ | $3$ |  $7$ | $5$ |  $5$ | $3$ |  $7$ |

</div>

The supplementary laws give the first three entries. For the last four, the combined residues modulo $24$ are respectively $5,5,11,23$; quadratic reciprocity gives $(d_0/p)=-1$ in each case. Odd primes dividing $M$ but not $d_0$ occur only in the square factor $t^2$ and can be assigned any allowed residue. The Chinese remainder theorem combines these choices into a class $u\pmod M$.

If $u\equiv3$ or $7\pmod8$, take $c=2$; if $u\equiv5\pmod8$, take $c=4$. In each case $(u-1)/c$ is odd. For every odd $\ell\mid M$, the condition $u\not\equiv1\pmod\ell$ also makes $(u-1)/c$ nonzero modulo $\ell$. This proves <a href="#alg:progression-conditions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:progression-conditions">[alg:progression-conditions]</a>. Finally, $(a/p)=(d_0/p)=-1$, since $p\nmid t$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-alg-quadratic-progression-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-alg-quadratic-progression</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:primitive-root" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:primitive-root">[thm:primitive-root]</a>.* Fix $a$ as in the theorem, so $|a|>1$. Choose $M,c,u$ by Lemma <a href="#alg:quadratic-progression" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="alg:quadratic-progression">6</a>, and take the $\gg_a x/L^2$ primes supplied by Proposition <a href="#prop:controlled" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:controlled">[prop:controlled]</a>. For each such prime $p$, Euler’s criterion gives $a^{(p-1)/2}\equiv-1\pmod p$. Consequently $i_p(a)$ is odd: an even index would make $\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_p(a)$ divide $(p-1)/2$.

If $i_p(a)>1$, choose a prime divisor $q$ of this index. Since $p-1=crQ$ and $c$ is a power of $2$, we have $q\mid rQ$. Moreover $\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_p(a)\mid(p-1)/q$, so <a href="#eq:kummer-frobenius-test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kummer-frobenius-test">[eq:kummer-frobenius-test]</a> holds. We bound the number of such primes $p$ in the two possible ranges of $q$.

If $q=Q$, then $$1\le\frac{p-1}{Q}<2x^{0.1}.$$ Thus $p$ divides the positive integer $$B_a(x)=\prod_{1\le j\le2x^{0.1}}|a^j-1|.$$ No factor vanishes because $|a|>1$, and $$\log B_a(x)
 \le\sum_{1\le j\le2x^{0.1}}\bigl(j\log|a|+\log2\bigr)
 \ll_a x^{0.2}.$$ Every distinct prime divisor $p>x$ contributes at least $L$ to this logarithm. The number of primes lost in this case is therefore $O_a(x^{0.2}/L)=o(x/L^2)$.

If $q\mid r$, then $\exp(L^{0.1})<q<\exp(L^{0.3})$. For large $x$, all these primes are sufficiently large for Proposition <a href="#prop:splitting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:splitting">4</a>; also $p\nmid aq$. Lemma <a href="#alg:splitting-test" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="alg:splitting-test">5</a> and a union bound give at most <a id="alg:exceptional-small-divisors"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 &\ll_a \frac{x}{L}
       \sum_{m>\exp(L^{0.1})}\frac1{m(m-1)}
       +x^{1-\delta_0}\exp(L^{0.3})\notag\\
 &\ll_a \frac{x}{L}\exp(-L^{0.1})
       +x\exp(-\delta_0L+L^{0.3})
       =o(x/L^2)
 \label{alg:exceptional-small-divisors}
\end{aligned}$$ primes, where the first sum is over integers. The first estimate uses <a href="#eq:uniform-splitting" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:uniform-splitting">[eq:uniform-splitting]</a> for each possible $q$; the second uses the telescoping sum of $1/(m(m-1))$.

Subtracting these two losses leaves $\gg_a x/L^2$ primes with $i_p(a)=1$. All choices depend only on $a$, and both estimates hold for every sufficiently large real $x$. This proves the theorem. ◻

</details>

For $a=2$, the theorem gives $\gg x/L^2$ primes in $(x,2x)$ with primitive root $2$, with absolute constants. This case can use the particularly simple choice $(M,c,u)=(8,4,5)$ in Proposition <a href="#prop:controlled" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:controlled">[prop:controlled]</a>: the predecessors are $4rQ$, and $p\equiv5\pmod8$ gives $(2/p)=-1$ directly.

<a id="stmt-auto-remark-1"></a>
<div class="remark">

*Remark 7*. Negative squares are covered by the row $d_0=-1$ in the progression lemma. Odd perfect powers also require no separate exclusion. If $a=b^h$ with fixed odd $h>1$, every prime divisor of $h$ is eventually smaller than every prime factor of $rQ$; hence $(h,p-1)=1$ for the constructed primes. This explains directly how the controlled predecessor removes the obstruction from the fixed exponent.

</div>

<a id="sec:arithmetic"></a>
### S-integers, residue symbols, and Gauss sums

The analytic argument will sum over ideals while also using their generators in local fields. We first choose generators for which reciprocity has no supplementary factor. We then identify the two Hecke characters which account for the phases of the cubic and sextic Gauss sums.

Fix a cyclotomic field $F$ containing $\mu_{12}$, and put $[F:\mathbb{Q}]=2r$. At a finite place $v$, normalize the absolute value by $|\varpi_v|_v=(\mathrm{N}v)^{-1}$; at a complex place use the square of the ordinary modulus. These are the absolute values in the product formula. Let $$\psi=\prod_v\psi_v:\mathbb{A}_F/F\longrightarrow \mathbb{C}^\times$$ be the standard trace additive character. We also fix a finite-order Hecke character $\eta$. Choose a finite set $S$ containing the complex places, all places above $6$, the finite conductor of $\eta$, and every place at which $\mathcal O_v$ is not self-dual for the pairing $\psi_v(xy)$. The theta normalization in [prop:theta-input](#prop:theta-input) and the local Euler-product estimate in [prop:euler-product](#prop:euler-product) require two additional fixed finite sets of excluded places. Include both sets in $S$. Their existence is established at those points, independently of the summation variables and of the arithmetic choices below. We may include any further fixed finite set of places. Enlarging $S$ by finitely many primes which generate the ideal class group, we arrange that the ring of S-integers $$R=\mathcal O_S=\{a\in F:v_{\mathfrak p}(a)\geq0
                       \text{ for every }\mathfrak p\notin S\}$$ is principal. This is the final choice of $S$, and all arithmetic data below are constructed for it before the coefficient family is defined. All assertions in this section concern this fixed $F$ and $S$; their auxiliary constants may depend on them.

Write $S_f$ for the finite places in $S$, $s_f=|S_f|$, and $$U_S=R^\times,\qquad F_S=\prod_{v\in S}F_v,\qquad
 |y|_S=\prod_{v\in S}|y_v|_v,\qquad
 F_S^1=\{y\in F_S^\times:|y|_S=1\}.$$ An *exterior ideal* is a nonzero integral ideal of $R$, equivalently an ideal supported at primes outside $S$. Its norm is $q_{\mathfrak a}=\mathrm{N}\mathfrak a=|R/\mathfrak a|$, extended multiplicatively to fractional ideals. Roman letters will denote generators of the corresponding fraktur ideals. For $a\in F^\times$, put $q_a=|a|_S$. Finally, on the additive group $F_S$ set $$\mathrm{e}(x)=\prod_{v\in S}\psi_v(x_v),
 \qquad
 \widehat f(y)=\int_{F_S}f(x)\mathrm{e}(-xy)\,dx,$$ where $dx$ is the product of the self-dual local additive measures.

<a id="stmt-lem-s-lattice"></a>
<div id="lem:s-lattice" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8** (The S-integer lattice). *The diagonal copy of $R$ is a discrete cocompact subgroup of $F_S$, is its own annihilator for the pairing $\mathrm{e}(xy)$, and has covolume $1$. For every Bruhat–Schwartz function $f$ on $F_S$, $$\sum_{m\in R}f(m)=\sum_{\nu\in R}\widehat f(\nu).$$ If $\mathfrak a=aR$ is an exterior fractional ideal, then $$q_a=q_{\mathfrak a},\qquad
 (aR)^\perp=a^{-1}R,\qquad
 \sum_{m\in aR}f(m)
   =q_{\mathfrak a}^{-1}\sum_{\nu\in a^{-1}R}\widehat f(\nu).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-s-lattice"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-s-lattice</summary>

*Proof.* Put $K^S=\prod_{v\notin S}\mathcal O_v$, embedded in the adeles with zero S-components. At every place outside $S$, the defining conductor condition says that $\mathcal O_v$ is its own annihilator. Its self-dual measure is consequently $1$, and $\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{K^S}}=\mathbf{1}_{K^S}$. Adelic additive duality identifies the annihilator of $F$ in $\mathbb{A}_F$ with $F$, and the additive quotient $\mathbb{A}_F/F$ has volume $1$; see <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Weil64](#ref-Weil64), Chapter II, 29]. Strong approximation identifies $$F_S/R\simeq \mathbb{A}_F/(F+K^S).$$ The quotient is compact because $\mathbb{A}_F/F$ is compact. To check discreteness, choose a compact neighborhood $C$ of zero in $F_S$. The intersection $F\cap(C\times K^S)$ is finite because diagonal $F$ is discrete and closed in the adeles. Shrinking $C$ excludes the finitely many nonzero S-components in this intersection, so zero is isolated in $R$. Taking annihilators in the last quotient gives $$(F+K^S)^\perp=F\cap(F_S\times K^S)=R,$$ with $R$ diagonally embedded, so its annihilator in $F_S$ is indeed $R$. The covolume of a lattice and that of its annihilator are reciprocal under self-dual measure; hence this covolume is $1$.

More directly, apply adelic Poisson summation <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Weil64](#ref-Weil64), Chapter III, 41, equation (39)] to $f\otimes\mathbf{1}_{K^S}$. Only the elements of $R$ occur on either side, and the exterior factor is unchanged by Fourier transform. This gives the displayed Poisson identity with no scalar factor. Multiplication by $a$ has additive modulus $|a|_S$, so scaling this identity gives the formula for $aR$. The product formula gives $$|a|_S=\prod_{v\notin S}|a|_v^{-1}
      =\mathrm{N}(aR)=q_{\mathfrak a},$$ which finishes the proof. ◻

</details>

We use Hilbert symbols to remove the supplementary factor in sextic reciprocity. Choose the local reciprocity map $\operatorname{Art}_v$ in which a uniformizer acts as arithmetic Frobenius on an unramified extension. For $n\in\{2,3,6\}$, set $$(a,b)_{n,v}
   =\frac{\operatorname{Art}_v(b)(a^{1/n})}{a^{1/n}}.$$ This orders the two arguments oppositely to the convention in <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Neukirch99](#ref-Neukirch99), Chapter V, 3]. Thus, at a place of residue characteristic prime to $n$, for a unit $u$ and a uniformizer $\varpi$, $$(u,\varpi)_{n,v}\equiv u^{(\mathrm{N}v-1)/n}\pmod v.$$ The value is read in $\mu_n\subset F$. On $$\mathcal V=F_S^\times/F_S^{\times6}$$ define $H_S(x,y)=\prod_{v\in S}(x_v,y_v)_{6,v}$, and let $E$ be the image of $U_S$ in $\mathcal V$. For $T>0$, let $\ell_T\in F_S^\times$ have finite components $1$ and component $T^{1/(2r)}$ at every complex place. Then $|\ell_T|_S=T$.

<a id="stmt-lem-balanced-generators"></a>
<div id="lem:balanced-generators" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9** (Generators with fixed power classes). *The pairing $H_S$ on $\mathcal V$ is nondegenerate and alternating. The map $U_S/U_S^6\to\mathcal V$ is injective, its image $E$ satisfies $E^\perp=E$, and there is an isotropic subgroup $B$ such that $\mathcal V=E\oplus B$.*

*Every exterior fractional ideal $\mathfrak a$ has a generator $a$ whose class $[a]$ in $\mathcal V$ belongs to $B$. Any two such generators differ by an element of $U_S^6$. They can be chosen so that $$a/\ell_{q_{\mathfrak a}}\in\mathcal K_{\mathrm{bal}}$$ for one fixed compact subset $\mathcal K_{\mathrm{bal}}\subset F_S^1$, independent of $\mathfrak a$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-balanced-generators"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-balanced-generators</summary>

*Proof.* For $\ell=2,3$, put $\mathcal V_\ell=F_S^\times/F_S^{\times\ell}$. Local Kummer theory and local reciprocity give a nondegenerate Hilbert pairing on this group <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Neukirch99](#ref-Neukirch99), Chapter V, Proposition (3.2)]. At a finite place $v$, $$\dim_{\mathbb{F}_\ell}F_v^\times/F_v^{\times\ell}
  =2+\mathbf{1}_{v\mid\ell}[F_v:\mathbb{Q}_\ell].$$ Indeed the valuation contributes one dimension. When $v\nmid\ell$, the residue units contribute one dimension and the principal units are $\ell$-divisible. When $v\mid\ell$, the principal units have a $\mathbb{Z}_\ell$-part of rank $[F_v:\mathbb{Q}_\ell]$ and an $\ell$-power root-of-unity part contributing one more dimension. The latter is present because $F$ contains $\mu_\ell$. This is also the local power-class formula <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Neukirch99](#ref-Neukirch99), Chapter II, Corollary (5.8)]. Complex power-class groups are trivial. All places above $\ell$ lie in $S$, so their local degrees sum to $2r$ and <a id="eq:local-power-dimension"></a>$$\dim_{\mathbb{F}_\ell}\mathcal V_\ell=2s_f+2r.\label{eq:local-power-dimension}$$ The S-unit theorem <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Neukirch99](#ref-Neukirch99), Chapter I, Corollary (11.7)] gives $U_S\simeq\mu(F)\times\mathbb{Z}^{r+s_f-1}$. Since $\mu(F)$ is cyclic and contains $\mu_\ell$, <a id="eq:unit-power-dimension"></a>$$\dim_{\mathbb{F}_\ell}U_S/U_S^\ell=r+s_f.\label{eq:unit-power-dimension}$$

We next check that the latter group injects into $\mathcal V_\ell$. Suppose that $u\in U_S$ is an $\ell$-th power in every $F_v$ with $v\in S$. The Kummer extension $F(u^{1/\ell})/F$ is cyclic and split at $S$. At every exterior prime $u$ is a unit and $\ell$ is invertible, so this extension is unramified there. It is consequently an everywhere unramified abelian extension in which every S-prime splits. The Hilbert class-field correspondence, together with the quotient description of the S-class group, identifies such extensions with quotients of the class group of $R$, which is trivial <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Neukirch99](#ref-Neukirch99), Chapter I, Proposition (11.6); Chapter VI, Proposition (6.9) and Theorem (7.3)]. Hence $u=t^\ell$ for $t\in F$. Its exterior valuations show $t\in U_S$, proving the injection.

For two S-units, every exterior Hilbert symbol is $1$, since both arguments are units and the residue characteristic is prime to $6$. Hilbert reciprocity <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Neukirch99](#ref-Neukirch99), Chapter VI, Theorem (8.1)] therefore makes their S-product $1$. The Hilbert pairing is alternating here: $-1=\zeta_{12}^6$, and the diagonal identity $(x,x)_{n,v}=(x,-1)_{n,v}$ makes its diagonal trivial for $n=2,3,6$. By [eq:local-power-dimension](#eq:local-power-dimension) and [eq:unit-power-dimension](#eq:unit-power-dimension), the injected unit image in $\mathcal V_\ell$ is an isotropic subspace of half the dimension. Nondegeneracy implies that it is self-orthogonal. Symplectic linear algebra supplies an isotropic complementary subspace for each $\ell$. The Chinese remainder isomorphism $\mathcal V\simeq\mathcal V_2\oplus\mathcal V_3$, under which the two pairings are the third and second powers of $H_S$, combines these complements into $B$. It also gives the asserted injection and self-orthogonality for exponent $6$.

Let $\mathfrak a=a_0R$. Decompose $[a_0]=e b$ in $E\oplus B$, and choose an S-unit representing $e^{-1}$. Its product with $a_0$ is a generator $a$ with $[a]\in B$. If $a'$ is another, then $a'/a$ is an S-unit whose class lies in $E\cap B$. It is therefore locally a sixth power at $S$, and the injection already proved says $a'/a\in U_S^6$.

Finally $a/\ell_{q_{\mathfrak a}}$ belongs to $F_S^1$, by [lem:s-lattice](#lem:s-lattice). To see the relevant compactness explicitly, the norm-one idele class group is compact <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Neukirch99](#ref-Neukirch99), Chapter VI, Theorem (1.6)]. The subgroup $F_S^1\times\prod_{v\notin S}\mathcal O_v^\times$ is open in the norm-one ideles and meets diagonal $F^\times$ in $U_S$. Its quotient by $U_S$ is therefore an open, hence closed, subgroup of that compact idele class group. Its projection onto $F_S^1/U_S$ is surjective, so $F_S^1/U_S$ is compact. Since $U_S^6$ has finite index in $U_S$, $F_S^1/U_S^6$ is compact as well. Choosing a compact set which maps onto this quotient and multiplying $a$ by a suitable element of $U_S^6$ proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

Call a generator with class in $B$ *allowed*, and call it *balanced* when it satisfies the compactness condition in [lem:balanced-generators](#lem:balanced-generators). We use balanced representatives for estimates involving S-components. In multiplicative identities we may instead use the product of the allowed generators of the prime factors: the product remains allowed and differs from the selected representative by an element of $U_S^6$.

For an exterior prime $\mathfrak p$, the reduction of $\mu_6$ into $(R/\mathfrak p)^\times$ is injective. Define the sextic residue character by $$\chi_{\mathfrak p}(x)\equiv x^{(q_{\mathfrak p}-1)/6}\pmod{\mathfrak p}
 \quad (x\not\equiv0\pmod{\mathfrak p}),\qquad
 \chi_{\mathfrak p}(0)=0.$$ For every integer $j$, the notation $\chi_{\mathfrak p}(x)^j$ means the usual character power on units and means $0$ on nonunits, also when $j\equiv0\pmod6$. If $\mathfrak a=\prod_{\mathfrak p}\mathfrak p^{k_{\mathfrak p}}$, put $$\chi_{\mathfrak a}(x)=
 \prod_{\mathfrak p\mid\mathfrak a}
       \chi_{\mathfrak p}(x)^{k_{\mathfrak p}},
 \qquad \chi_R(x)=1.$$ The same zero convention applies to powers of $\chi_{\mathfrak a}$.

For distinct exterior primes with allowed generators $p,p'$, global reciprocity gives the exact equality <a id="eq:sextic-reciprocity"></a>$$\chi_{\mathfrak p}(p')=\chi_{\mathfrak p'}(p).
\label{eq:sextic-reciprocity}$$ Indeed the only possibly nontrivial exterior symbols in the product formula for $(p,p')_6$ are $(p,p')_{6,\mathfrak p}=\chi_{\mathfrak p}(p')^{-1}$ and $(p,p')_{6,\mathfrak p'}=\chi_{\mathfrak p'}(p)$. The S-product is $1$ because $B$ is isotropic. If $m\in R\setminus\{0\}$ and $\mathfrak b=(m)R$, write $m=\epsilon b$ with $b$ allowed and $\epsilon\in U_S$. Multiplying [eq:sextic-reciprocity](#eq:sextic-reciprocity) over prime factors gives, for an allowed generator $a$ of $\mathfrak a$, <a id="eq:generator-reciprocity"></a>$$\chi_{\mathfrak a}(m)
  =\chi_{\mathfrak a}(\epsilon)\chi_{\mathfrak b}(a).
\label{eq:generator-reciprocity}$$ This includes the case $(\mathfrak a,\mathfrak b)\ne R$: both sides are then zero. For an S-unit $\epsilon$, the same product formula gives <a id="eq:unit-symbol"></a>$$\chi_{\mathfrak a}(\epsilon)=H_S(\epsilon,a)^{-1}.
\label{eq:unit-symbol}$$ Thus its dependence on $\mathfrak a$ is through the finite class $[a]$.

<a id="stmt-lem-kummer-characters"></a>
<div id="lem:kummer-characters" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10** (Kummer and class characters). *For $u\in R\setminus\{0\}$, the ideal function $$\chi_\bullet(u)(\mathfrak a)=\chi_{\mathfrak a}(u)$$ is induced away from $S$ and the prime divisors of $(u)R$ by a finite-order Hecke character. Its values at those prime divisors are set equal to zero. If $\mathfrak f_u$ is the conductor of the inducing primitive character, then <a id="eq:kummer-conductor"></a>$$\mathrm{N}\mathfrak f_u\le C_{F,S}\mathrm{N}\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}((u)R)
                       \le C_{F,S}q_u.\label{eq:kummer-conductor}$$ The same bound, with a changed constant, holds after twisting by the fixed $\eta$. The inducing characters associated to $u$ and $v$ agree if and only if $u/v\in F^{\times6}$.*

*Every character of the finite group $B$, evaluated on allowed generators, extends to a finite-order Hecke character unramified outside $S$.*

*The following parametrization will index the nonzero Poisson frequencies; its character distinctness and conductor bound will be used in zero detection. Choose a complete set of representatives $\mathcal U$ modulo $U_S^6$ among the nonzero S-integers whose exterior valuations belong to $\{0,1,\ldots,5\}$, with $1\in\mathcal U$. For allowed generators $r$, the map <a id="eq:frequency-decomposition"></a>$$(u,\mathfrak r)\longmapsto [u r^6]
 \quad\text{from }\mathcal U\times\{\text{exterior ideals}\}
 \quad\text{to }(R\setminus\{0\})/U_S^6\label{eq:frequency-decomposition}$$ is a bijection. Among $u\in\mathcal U$, only $u=1$ induces the principal character, and distinct $u$’s induce distinct characters, also after the fixed twist by $\eta$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-kummer-characters"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-kummer-characters</summary>

*Proof.* Because $\mu_6\subset F$, Kummer theory <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Neukirch99](#ref-Neukirch99), Chapter IV, Theorem (3.3) and Corollary (3.6)] identifies $u\in F^\times$ with the character $$\kappa_u(\sigma)=\frac{\sigma(u^{1/6})}{u^{1/6}}
 \quad\text{of }\mathop{\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline F/F),$$ whose kernel as a function of $u$ is exactly $F^{\times6}$. Global class-field theory identifies this finite abelian character with a finite-order Hecke character <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Neukirch99](#ref-Neukirch99), Chapter VI, Theorem (5.5) and Proposition (5.6)]. At an exterior prime $\mathfrak p\nmid(u)R$, arithmetic Frobenius satisfies $$\kappa_u(\operatorname{Frob}_{\mathfrak p})
 \equiv (u^{1/6})^{q_{\mathfrak p}-1}
 =u^{(q_{\mathfrak p}-1)/6}
 =\chi_{\mathfrak p}(u)\pmod{\mathfrak p}.$$ Reduction is injective on $\mu_6$, so these are equal as roots of unity. This proves the asserted identification on good primes.

At an exterior place, the principal units are sixth powers by Hensel’s lemma. Hence the associated local Hilbert character is trivial on principal units, giving conductor exponent at most one. The tame Hilbert formula <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[Neukirch99](#ref-Neukirch99), Chapter V, Proposition (3.4)] shows that the pairing of two units is $1$, so if $u$ is a local unit the character is unramified. At a place in $S$, it depends only on the class of $u$ in the finite group $F_v^\times/F_v^{\times6}$; the finitely many resulting local conductors have a fixed maximum. This proves [eq:kummer-conductor](#eq:kummer-conductor). A fixed twist supported in $S$ only changes that maximum. Equality of two inducing Hecke characters is equality of their Kummer characters, since unramified Frobenius elements determine a finite abelian character. The Kummer kernel gives the stated criterion. Setting extra prime values to zero merely omits Euler factors and does not alter the inducing character.

For a character $\theta_B$ of $B$, extend it to $\mathcal V=E\oplus B$ by $\theta_S(eb)=\theta_B(b)$. This is a continuous finite-order character of $F_S^\times$ trivial on $U_S$. If $\mathfrak a=aR$, define $\theta(\mathfrak a)=\theta_S(a)$. It is independent of the generator and multiplicative. At every exterior place take the unramified local character sending a uniformizer to $\theta(\mathfrak p)$, and at $S$ take $\theta_S^{-1}$. Their product is trivial on diagonal $F^\times$ by construction and is the required Hecke character.

To prove [eq:frequency-decomposition](#eq:frequency-decomposition), divide each exterior valuation of a nonzero $h$ by $6$. This determines an ideal $\mathfrak r$ and the exterior valuations of an S-integer $u_0=h/r^6$ in $\{0,\ldots,5\}$. Replacing $u_0$ by its representative in $\mathcal U$ changes $u_0r^6$ only by an element of $U_S^6$. Conversely, the exterior valuations of $[ur^6]$ determine $\mathfrak r$ and those of $u$; equality of two such classes then says that the two representatives $u$ differ by $U_S^6$. Changing an allowed $r$ by $U_S^6$ does not affect its image in the quotient. This proves bijectivity.

If $u\in\mathcal U$ induces the principal character, the first part gives $u=t^6$ in $F$. Its exterior valuations are divisible by $6$ and between $0$ and $5$, hence are all zero; the same is true for the exterior valuations of $t$. Thus $u\in U_S^6$, giving $u=1$. If $u,v\in\mathcal U$ induce the same character, the differences of their exterior valuations are divisible by $6$ and lie in $[-5,5]$, so vanish. Their quotient is then a sixth power of an S-unit, and $u=v$. The fixed twist by $\eta$ cancels from an equality of twisted characters. ◻

</details>

We now determine the phases of the Gauss sums. If $\mathfrak c$ is a squarefree exterior ideal with allowed generator $c$, define <a id="eq:normalized-gauss"></a>$$\gamma_j(\mathfrak c)
  =q_{\mathfrak c}^{-1/2}
       \sum_{x\bmod\mathfrak c}\chi_{\mathfrak c}(x)^j\mathrm{e}(x/c),
 \qquad \gamma_j(R)=1.\label{eq:normalized-gauss}$$ The additive character on $R/\mathfrak c$ here is primitive by [lem:s-lattice](#lem:s-lattice). A change $c\mapsto\epsilon^6c$, with $\epsilon\in U_S$, changes the additive argument by a sixth power and is absorbed by a change of residue variable; hence the sum is independent of the allowed generator. At an exterior prime, the adelic product formula gives $$\mathrm{e}(x/p)=\psi_{\mathfrak p}(-x/p).$$ The reduction of $\mu_{12}$ is injective there, so $q_{\mathfrak p}\equiv1\pmod{12}$ and $\chi_{\mathfrak p}(-1)=1$. The minus sign can therefore be suppressed in all these prime Gauss sums.

<a id="stmt-lem-gauss-characters"></a>
<div id="lem:gauss-characters" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11** (The Gauss correction characters). *There are a unitary Hecke character $\Lambda$, unramified outside $S$, and a finite-order Hecke character $G$, unramified outside $S$, such that for every exterior prime $\mathfrak p$, <a id="eq:gauss-corrections"></a>$$\gamma_2(\mathfrak p)^3=-\Lambda(\mathfrak p),\qquad
 G(\mathfrak p)=\overline{\chi_{\mathfrak p}(4)}\,
                     \gamma_3(\mathfrak p),\qquad
 \gamma_1(\mathfrak p)\gamma_2(\mathfrak p)
                      =-\Lambda(\mathfrak p)G(\mathfrak p).
\label{eq:gauss-corrections}$$ At each complex place the infinity type of $\Lambda$ is purely angular of exponent $1$ or $-1$, in the ordinary angular coordinate. The values $\gamma_3(\mathfrak p)$ are signs and depend multiplicatively on the class of an allowed $p$ in $B$.*

*For coprime squarefree exterior ideals, <a id="eq:gauss-crt"></a>$$\gamma_j(\mathfrak c_1\mathfrak c_2)
 =\chi_{\mathfrak c_1}(c_2)^j
  \chi_{\mathfrak c_2}(c_1)^j
  \gamma_j(\mathfrak c_1)\gamma_j(\mathfrak c_2).
\label{eq:gauss-crt}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-gauss-characters"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-gauss-characters</summary>

*Proof.* First work over a finite field $k$ of size $Q$, with a nontrivial additive character $\psi_k$, a character $\chi$ of exact order $6$, and $\rho=\chi^2$. Write $$G_j=\sum_{x\in k}\chi(x)^j\psi_k(x),\qquad
 J(\alpha,\beta)=\sum_{x\in k}\alpha(x)\beta(1-x).$$ For nontrivial characters the usual Gauss inversion and Gauss–Jacobi identities give <a id="eq:cubic-jacobi"></a>$$G_2G_4=Q,\qquad G_2^2=J(\rho,\rho)G_4,\qquad
 (Q^{-1/2}G_2)^3=Q^{-1/2}J(\rho,\rho).\label{eq:cubic-jacobi}$$ The first equality uses $\rho(-1)=1$. Changing $\psi_k(x)$ to $\psi_k(ax)$ multiplies $G_j$ by $\overline{\chi(a)}^{\,j}$, so the cube in [eq:cubic-jacobi](#eq:cubic-jacobi) is independent of the additive character.

Let $E_0=\mathbb{Q}(\omega)$, where $\omega^2+\omega+1=0$. Every ideal of $\mathbb{Z}[\omega]$ prime to $3$ has a unique generator congruent to $1$ modulo $3$; denote it by $\pi_+(\mathfrak a)$. This follows from class number one and the bijection of the six units with $(\mathbb{Z}[\omega]/3)^\times$, and is the convention used in <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[BaierYoung10](#ref-BaierYoung10), 2.1]. For a prime $\mathfrak q\nmid6$ with residue size $Q$, take $\rho(x)\equiv x^{(Q-1)/3}\pmod{\mathfrak q}$. Reduction of the Jacobi sum gives $$J(\rho,\rho)\equiv
 \sum_{x\in\mathbb{F}_Q}x^{(Q-1)/3}(1-x)^{(Q-1)/3}=0
 \pmod{\mathfrak q},$$ since every monomial has degree strictly between $0$ and $Q-1$. The Gauss identities also give $|J(\rho,\rho)|^2=Q$. To determine the unit, put $t=1-\omega$; the ideals $(t^2)$ and $(3)$ are equal. Writing $\rho(x)=\omega^{k(x)}$, with $k(x)\in\{0,1,2\}$, and expanding modulo $t^2$ yields $$J(\rho,\rho)\equiv Q-2
   -t\sum_{x\ne0,1}\bigl(k(x)+k(1-x)\bigr)
 \equiv Q-2-2(Q-1)t\equiv-1\pmod3.$$ Here each value of $\rho$ occurs $(Q-1)/3$ times on $\mathbb{F}_Q^\times$, and $Q\equiv1\pmod3$. Divisibility by $\mathfrak q$, the norm equality, and this congruence show <a id="eq:eisenstein-jacobi"></a>$$J(\rho,\rho)=-\pi_+(\mathfrak q).
\label{eq:eisenstein-jacobi}$$ This is also the prime case of the classical cubic Gauss identity $g(n)^3=\mu(n)\mathrm{N}(n)n$ in <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[BaierYoung10](#ref-BaierYoung10), 2.2, equation (11) in arXiv:0804.2233v4].

Define on ideals of $E_0$ prime to $3$ $$\Lambda_{E_0}(\mathfrak a)
     =\frac{\pi_+(\mathfrak a)}{|\pi_+(\mathfrak a)|}.$$ The generators congruent to $1\pmod3$ multiply, so this is a multiplicative ideal character. On a principal ideal generated by $a\equiv1\pmod3$ its value is $a/|a|$; thus it is the unitary Hecke character of finite conductor dividing $(3)$ and angular infinity type of exponent $1$, in ideal-character convention. Set $\Lambda=\Lambda_{E_0}\circ\mathrm{N}_{F/E_0}$. If $\mathfrak p$ lies above $\mathfrak q$ with residue degree $f$, the power definition of the residue character shows that the cubic character at $\mathfrak p$ is the lift of that at $\mathfrak q$ by the residue-field norm. The Davenport–Hasse Jacobi lifting formula says $$J_{\mathbb{F}_{Q^f}}(\rho\circ\mathrm{N},\rho\circ\mathrm{N})
       =(-1)^{f-1}J_{\mathbb{F}_Q}(\rho,\rho)^f;$$ its hypotheses hold because $\rho$, $\rho$, and $\rho^2$ are all nontrivial <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[HoshiKanai22](#ref-HoshiKanai22), Theorem 1.1]. By [eq:eisenstein-jacobi](#eq:eisenstein-jacobi), the right side is $-\pi_+(\mathfrak q)^f$. Since $\mathrm{N}_{F/E_0}\mathfrak p=\mathfrak q^f$, [eq:cubic-jacobi](#eq:cubic-jacobi) proves $\gamma_2(\mathfrak p)^3=-\Lambda(\mathfrak p)$.
