<details id="proof-stmt-con-harmonic"><summary>Proof of stmt-con-harmonic</summary>

*Proof.* At a prime $p$, summing over its positive exponents contributes $\sum_{j\ge1}p^{-j}=1/(p-1)$. Consequently $E_i(t)$ is the harmonic generating function for integers supported on the $i$th group, with $t$ recording their number of distinct prime factors. Differentiating its finite product and evaluating at $t=1/2$ inserts the factor $\omega_i(r)(1/2)^{\omega_i(r)-1}$. The groups are disjoint, so multiplying these marked sums and dividing by $\prod_iV_i$ proves <a href="#con:harmonic-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:harmonic-identity">[con:harmonic-identity]</a>.

Integers with one prime, to exponent one, from each group contribute $$\prod_{i=1}^K\left(V_i^{-1}\sum_{p\in\mathcal P_i}p^{-1}\right)=1$$ to $J_0$. For the upper bound and tail put $s=L^{-a_K}$. Every group prime satisfies $p^s\le\mathrm{e}^2$, so, for large $x$, $$\sum_{j\ge1}p^{-j(1-s)}\le\frac{2\mathrm{e}^2}{p}.$$ Bounding the mark by $B_K$ and multiplying the geometric series over group primes gives $$\sum_r\frac{\mathcal W(r)}{r^{1-s}}\ll_K1,$$ since $\sum_iV_i\ll K$. Multiplication by $x^{-\varepsilon s}$ on $r>x^\varepsilon$ proves the tail. Finally, $A_r\ll x/r$ by counting integers in the interval for $Q$ whenever $A_r\ne0$; otherwise the bound is immediate. ◻

</details>

Take temporarily <a id="con:small-parameters"></a>$$\label{con:small-parameters}
 0<\kappa<0.01,\qquad 0<\varepsilon<\kappa,
 \qquad 0<b<0.01,
 \qquad D=x^{1/2-\kappa/2}.$$ For an odd squarefree integer $\ell$, let $A_r(\ell)$ denote the sum defining $A_r$ with the additional condition $\ell\mid crQ+1$, and set <a id="con:local-density"></a>$$\label{con:local-density}
 g_r(\ell)=\frac{\mathbf{1}_{\{(\ell,Mr)=1\}}}{\varphi(\ell)},\qquad
 E_r(\ell)=A_r(\ell)-g_r(\ell)A_r.$$ In particular, $g_r(1)=1$ and $E_r(1)=0$.

<a id="stmt-con-distribution"></a>
<div id="con:distribution" class="lemma">

<a id="con:bv-sum"></a>
<a id="con:mass-asymptotic"></a>
<a id="con:total-mass"></a>
**Lemma 36** (Distribution of the weighted family). *For every fixed $A>0$, $$\label{con:bv-sum}
 \sum_{r\le x^\varepsilon}\mathcal W(r)
 \sum_{\substack{\ell\le D\\\ell\text{ odd squarefree}}}|E_r(\ell)|
       \ll xJ_0L^{-A}.$$ Moreover, writing $A_\Psi=\int_1^2\Psi(y)\,dy$, we have $$\label{con:mass-asymptotic}
 X_0\sim\frac{xJ_0A_\Psi}{c\varphi(M/c)L}
 \qquad(x\to\infty),$$ with all construction parameters fixed and $J_0=J_0(x)$. In particular, $$\label{con:total-mass}
 X_0\asymp_{M,\Psi}\frac{xJ_0}{L},$$ with comparison constants independent of $K,\kappa,\varepsilon,b$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-con-distribution"><summary>Proof of stmt-con-distribution</summary>

*Proof.* Put $N=M/c$. For $r\le x^\varepsilon$, the support of $\Psi$ makes $Q>x^{0.9}$ automatic. The progression for $d$ is equivalent to the reduced class $$Q\equiv\frac{u-1}{c}\,r^{-1}\pmod N.$$ If $(\ell,Mr)>1$, then $A_r(\ell)=g_r(\ell)=0$. Otherwise the additional condition is $Q\equiv-(cr)^{-1}\pmod\ell$, and the two classes combine to one reduced class modulo $N\ell$.

<a id="con:bv-input"></a>
We use the prime-counting form of the Bombieri–Vinogradov theorem <a id="pm-cite-65"></a>[[Bombieri65](#ref-Bombieri65), [Vinogradov65](#ref-Vinogradov65)]: for fixed $A',\eta>0$, $$\label{con:bv-input}
 \sum_{q<Y^{1/2-\eta}}\max_{(v,q)=1}
 \left|\pi(Y;q,v)-\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Li}}(Y)}{\varphi(q)}\right|
       \ll_{A',\eta}Y(\log Y)^{-A'}.$$ Here $\mathop{\mathrm{Li}}(Y)=\int_2^Ydt/\log t$. Uniformly for $r\le x^\varepsilon$ and $Y\asymp x/(cr)$, the moduli $N\ell$, $\ell\le D$, lie in this range. Indeed, with $\eta=(\kappa-\varepsilon)/4$, $$(1-\varepsilon)(1/2-\eta)-(1/2-\kappa/2)
       =\frac{(\kappa-\varepsilon)(1+\varepsilon)}4>0,$$ which also absorbs the fixed factor $N$.

Define $$I_r=\frac{x}{cr}\int_1^2
               \frac{\Psi(y)}{\log((xy-1)/(cr))}\,dy.$$ For every fixed $0<\theta<0.1$, the support of $\Psi$ forces $Q>x^{0.9}$ whenever $r\le x^\theta$. The prime number theorem in the fixed reduced progressions modulo $N$, followed by partial summation, therefore gives, for every fixed $A>0$, <a id="con:fixed-r-mass"></a>$$\label{con:fixed-r-mass}
 A_r=\frac{I_r}{\varphi(N)}
      +O_{A,M,\Psi}\bigl((x/r)L^{-A}\bigr)
 \qquad(r\le x^\theta).$$ The class varies with $r$, but $N$ is fixed and every class is reduced. The implied constant is independent of $\theta$, although the threshold for the automatic cutoff may depend on $\theta$. This single-modulus estimate thus holds throughout $0<\theta<0.1$; the restriction $\varepsilon<\kappa$ is needed for the joint modulus estimate that follows.

<a id="con:smoothed-bv"></a>
Partial summation in <a href="#con:bv-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:bv-input">[con:bv-input]</a> yields $$\label{con:smoothed-bv}
 \sum_{\substack{\ell\le D\\\ell\text{ odd squarefree}}}
 \left|A_r(\ell)-\frac{g_r(\ell)}{\varphi(N)}I_r\right|
       \ll_{A'}\frac xrL^{-A'}.$$ To check the smoothing uniformly, integrate the progression remainder against the derivative of $\Psi((crt+1)/x)$. Its total absolute integral is $\int|\Psi'(y)|\,dy$, independently of $r$, and all integration points have $t\asymp x/(cr)$. The moduli $N\ell$ are distinct, and the maximum in <a href="#con:bv-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:bv-input">[con:bv-input]</a> covers their varying residue classes.

The term $\ell=1$ of <a href="#con:smoothed-bv" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:smoothed-bv">[con:smoothed-bv]</a> evaluates $A_r$. Replacing $I_r/\varphi(N)$ by $A_r$ in the other terms costs at most one logarithmic factor, because $$\sum_{\ell\le D}g_r(\ell)\le\sum_{\ell\le D}\frac1{\varphi(\ell)}
       \ll L.$$ For example, expand $n/\varphi(n)=\sum_{d\mid n}\mu^2(d)/\varphi(d)$ and use $\sum_d\mu^2(d)/(d\varphi(d))<\infty$. Choosing $A'$ sufficiently large gives $\sum_{\ell\le D}|E_r(\ell)|\ll(x/r)L^{-A}$; summing with the marks proves <a href="#con:bv-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:bv-sum">[con:bv-sum]</a>.

On the support of $\Psi$, for $r\le x^\varepsilon$ the logarithm in $I_r$ is comparable to $L$, with absolute comparison constants since $\varepsilon<0.01$ and $c\in\{2,4\}$. Thus $I_r\asymp_\Psi x/(rL)$. Summing the evaluation of $A_r$ and using Lemma <a href="#con:harmonic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="con:harmonic">35</a> proves <a href="#con:total-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:total-mass">[con:total-mass]</a>. The tail divided by $xJ_0/L$ tends to zero since $J_0\ge1$. Although its threshold may depend on $K$, the resulting comparison constants do not.

To obtain the precise asymptotic, fix an auxiliary exponent $0<\theta<\varepsilon$, without changing the sieve parameters. Uniformly for $r\le x^\theta$ and $y$ in the support of $\Psi$, $$\log((xy-1)/(cr))=L+O(\theta L+1),$$ and hence $$I_r=\frac{xA_\Psi}{crL}
             \bigl(1+O(\theta)+O(L^{-1})\bigr).$$ The constants in these relative errors are independent of $\theta$. Use the $\ell=1$ term of <a href="#con:smoothed-bv" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:smoothed-bv">[con:smoothed-bv]</a> with $A'>2$, multiply by $\mathcal W(r)$, and sum over $r\le x^\theta$. The summed progression error is $O(xJ_0L^{-A'})$. Lemma <a href="#con:harmonic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="con:harmonic">35</a> bounds the omitted parts of both $X_0$ and $J_0$; after the harmonic tail is multiplied by $x/L$, both are $o_\theta(xJ_0/L)$, since $J_0\ge1$. Thus $$\frac{c\varphi(N)L X_0}{xJ_0A_\Psi}
       =1+O(\theta)+o_\theta(1).$$ First let $x$ tend to infinity with $\theta$ fixed, and then let $\theta$ decrease to zero. This proves <a href="#con:mass-asymptotic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:mass-asymptotic">[con:mass-asymptotic]</a>. For $(M,c,u)=(8,4,5)$, it becomes $X_0\sim xJ_0A_\Psi/(4L)$, since $\varphi(M/c)=\varphi(2)=1$. ◻

</details>

<a id="con:mertens"></a>
<a id="con:omitted-primes"></a>
To express the sieve products, define <a id="con:singular-product"></a>$$\label{con:singular-product}
 \begin{split}
 V_M(y)&=\prod_{\substack{2<p\le y\\p\nmid M}}
                            \left(1-\frac1{p-1}\right),\\
 \mathfrak S_M&=2\prod_{p>2}\left(1-\frac1{(p-1)^2}\right)
       \prod_{\substack{p\mid M\\p>2}}
                            \left(1-\frac1{p-1}\right)^{-1}>0.
 \end{split}$$ Division by $V(y)$ and Mertens’ theorem give $$\label{con:mertens}
 V_M(y)\sim\mathfrak S_M V(y),\qquad
 V(y)\sim\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma_E}}{\log y},$$ where $\gamma_E$ is Euler’s constant. The primes dividing any supported $r$ are large enough that their omission changes these products negligibly. More precisely, <a href="#con:support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:support">[con:support]</a> gives $$\label{con:omitted-primes}
 \sum_{p\mid r}\frac1p\le0.11L^{0.9}\exp(-L^{0.1})=o(1),
 \qquad
 \prod_{2<p\le y}(1-g_r(p))=(1+o(1))V_M(y),$$ uniformly for $r\le x^{0.11}$ and all $y$. The bound on the relative error is independent of $K$.

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
#### Two sieve estimates

The block sieve of Lemma <a href="#lem:block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:block">32</a>, from <a id="pm-cite-66"></a>[[BlockPaper](#ref-BlockPaper), Lemma 2.9], now gives the initial mass without small prime factors and a conditional mass for a specified divisor. Both estimates use the distribution already proved, but at different sieve depths.

<a id="con:initial-lower"></a>
First sieve the prime objects $Q$ in $A_r$, for $r\le x^\varepsilon$, by the conditions $p\mid crQ+1$ at odd primes $p\le z=x^b$. The local densities are $g_r(p)$, with $g_r(p)\le1/2$ and $\sum_{v<p\le v^2}g_r(p)\ll1$ with absolute constants. Choose the even depth $$H=2\left\lfloor\frac1{40b}\right\rfloor.$$ For sufficiently small fixed $b$, this is large enough for the lower bound in Lemma <a href="#lem:block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:block">32</a>; moreover, $$z^{4H+2}\le x^{0.2+2b}<D,\qquad
 \mathrm{e}^{-H}\le\mathrm{e}^2\exp(-1/(20b)).$$ All remainders are therefore covered by <a href="#con:bv-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:bv-sum">[con:bv-sum]</a>. Every supported $d$ is odd. Summing the sieve bound with the marks, using <a href="#con:mertens" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:mertens">[con:mertens]</a>–<a href="#con:omitted-primes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:omitted-primes">[con:omitted-primes]</a> and the negligible tail in Lemma <a href="#con:harmonic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="con:harmonic">35</a>, gives $$\label{con:initial-lower}
 S_b:=\sum_{P^-(d)>x^b}w(d)
 \ge\frac{\mathfrak S_M X_0}{L}
 \left\{\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma_E}}b
              (1-C_s\mathrm{e}^{-c_s/b})+o(1)\right\},$$ where $C_s,c_s>0$ are absolute; the displayed bound on $\mathrm{e}^{-H}$ permits $c_s=1/20$, after enlarging $C_s$. The remainders are $o(X_0/L)$ by taking $A>2$ in <a href="#con:bv-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:bv-sum">[con:bv-sum]</a>.

<a id="stmt-con-bin-mass"></a>
<div id="con:bin-mass" class="lemma">

<a id="con:bin-mass-formula"></a>
**Lemma 37** (Mass conditioned on a divisor). *For any fixed $b\le\gamma<\gamma'\le1/2-\kappa$, $$\label{con:bin-mass-formula}
 \sum_{\substack{x^\gamma<n\le x^{\gamma'}\\n\text{ prime}}}
 \sum_{\substack{n\mid d\\P^-(d)>W}}w(d)
 =\mathfrak S_M X_0 V(W)
                  \left(\log\frac{\gamma'}\gamma+o(1)\right).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-con-bin-mass"><summary>Proof of stmt-con-bin-mass</summary>

*Proof.* For large $x$, the primes $n$ in this sum exceed $W$ and all group primes, and do not divide $M$. Fix $r\le x^\varepsilon$ and condition the family in $A_r$ on $n\mid crQ+1$. For a squarefree product $\ell$ of odd primes at most $W$, the intersection count is $$A_r(n\ell)=\frac{A_r}{n-1}g_r(\ell)+E_r(n\ell).$$ This includes $\ell=1$, as allowed in Lemma <a href="#lem:block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:block">32</a>. Apply that lemma with main mass $A_r/(n-1)$ and $H=2\lceil(\log L)^2\rceil$. Its relative error tends to zero, and its remainder moduli satisfy $$\ell\le W^{4H+2}=\exp(O(L^{0.24}(\log L)^2))=x^{o(1)},
 \qquad n\ell\le x^{1/2-\kappa+o(1)}<D.$$ The map $(n,\ell)\mapsto n\ell$ is injective, since $n$ is the unique prime factor exceeding $W$. Thus the total remainder after summing $n$ and the marked $r$’s is bounded by a single copy of <a href="#con:bv-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:bv-sum">[con:bv-sum]</a>.

The sieve product is $(1+o(1))V_M(W)$ uniformly in $r$, and Mertens’ theorem gives $$\sum_{\substack{x^\gamma<n\le x^{\gamma'}\\n\text{ prime}}}
                      \frac1{n-1}=\log(\gamma'/\gamma)+o(1).$$ By <a href="#con:mass-definitions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:mass-definitions">[con:mass-definitions]</a>, Lemma <a href="#con:harmonic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="con:harmonic">35</a>, and <a href="#con:total-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:total-mass">[con:total-mass]</a>, for every fixed $A>0$ we have $$\sum_{r\le x^\varepsilon}\mathcal W(r)A_r
       =X_0+o(X_0L^{-A}).$$ This evaluates the contribution of $r\le x^\varepsilon$ with the exact main mass $X_0$; taking $A$ large in <a href="#con:bv-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:bv-sum">[con:bv-sum]</a> makes the remainders $o(X_0V(W))$. Finally, each supported $d$ has at most $\log(2x)/(bL)=O(1/b)$ distinct prime divisors above $x^b$. Consequently the harmonic tail bounds the omitted $r>x^\varepsilon$ contribution even after summing over $n$. This proves <a href="#con:bin-mass-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:bin-mass-formula">[con:bin-mass-formula]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
#### Removing composites by their least prime factor

We next pass from $S_b$ to $$U_\kappa=\sum_{P^-(d)>x^{1/2-\kappa}}w(d).$$ For a bin $(\gamma,\gamma']\subseteq(b,1/2-\kappa]$, with the first left endpoint allowed to equal $b$, define $$T_{\gamma,\gamma'}=
 \sum_{\substack{x^\gamma<n\le x^{\gamma'}\\n\text{ prime}}}
                    \sum_{P^-(m)>x^\gamma}w(mn).$$ If $x^\gamma<P^-(d)\le x^{\gamma'}$, taking $n=P^-(d)$ and $m=d/n$ shows that its weight is included in this sum. This remains true when $n^2\mid d$.

Partition both factors into disjoint half-open dyads with endpoints powers of two, retaining pairs that meet the smooth product support and the range of $n$. On that support, with $t=\log n/L$, $$\frac{\log m}{L}=1-t+O(L^{-1}).$$ Every retained full $m$-dyad therefore satisfies <a href="#con:exponent-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:exponent-range">[con:exponent-range]</a> with $$s_-=1/2+\kappa/2,\qquad s_+=1-b/2,$$ for large $x$; in particular $s_->\gamma$. Both dyad scales are at least $x^{b/2}$, and their product is comparable to $x$. There are $O(L)$ such pairs, since each $n$-dyad allows only a bounded number of $m$-dyads.

<a id="con:bin-cost"></a>
Apply Lemma <a href="#con:type-ii" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="con:type-ii">34</a> in every pair with $\delta=b/2$ and with $b_n$ the prime indicator restricted to its dyad and bin. These coefficients are $W$-rough. The total error is $O(xL^{-5})=o(X_0/L)$, by <a href="#con:total-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:total-mass">[con:total-mass]</a> and $J_0\ge1$. Hence $$T_{\gamma,\gamma'}=
 \sum_{\substack{x^\gamma<n\le x^{\gamma'}\\n\text{ prime}}}
 \sum_{m>x^\gamma}\frac{D_\gamma(\log m/L)}{LV(W)}
                         \mathbf{1}_{\{P^-(m)>W\}}w(mn)+o(X_0/L).$$ Joint continuity of the density on the fixed compact exponent range gives, on the support of the summand, $$D_\gamma(\log m/L)
       \le\sup_{\gamma\le t\le\gamma'}D_\gamma(1-t)+o(1).$$ Since $n>W$, the remaining roughness condition is precisely $P^-(mn)>W$. Lemma <a href="#con:bin-mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="con:bin-mass">37</a> thus gives $$\label{con:bin-cost}
 T_{\gamma,\gamma'}\le\frac{\mathfrak S_M X_0}{L}
 \left\{\sup_{\gamma\le t\le\gamma'}D_\gamma(1-t)
                       \log\frac{\gamma'}\gamma+o(1)\right\}.$$

<a id="con:density-budget"></a>
<a id="con:post-bin"></a>
The density estimate in Lemma <a href="#lem:density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:density">33</a> is normalized to match this expression: $$\label{con:density-budget}
 \int_b^{1/2}D_t(1-t)\frac{dt}{t}=D_b(1)-1,
 \qquad
 D_b(1)\le\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma_E}}b(1+C_d\mathrm{e}^{-c_d/b})$$ for sufficiently small fixed $b$. On $b\le\gamma\le t\le1/2-\kappa$, the point $(\gamma,1-t)$ stays a fixed distance from $s=\gamma$. Uniform continuity and the finite mass of $dt/t$ therefore give a sufficiently fine fixed partition $$b=\gamma_0<\gamma_1<\cdots<\gamma_J=1/2-\kappa$$ such that $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{j=1}^J
 \sup_{\gamma_{j-1}\le t\le\gamma_j}D_{\gamma_{j-1}}(1-t)
                         \log\frac{\gamma_j}{\gamma_{j-1}}
 &\le\int_b^{1/2-\kappa}D_t(1-t)\frac{dt}{t}+0.1\\
 &\le D_b(1)-1+0.1.
\end{aligned}$$ Subtracting <a href="#con:bin-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:bin-cost">[con:bin-cost]</a> for these finitely many bins from <a href="#con:initial-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:initial-lower">[con:initial-lower]</a> proves $$\label{con:post-bin}
 U_\kappa\ge\frac{\mathfrak S_M X_0}{L}
 \left\{0.9-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma_E}}b
       (C_s\mathrm{e}^{-c_s/b}+C_d\mathrm{e}^{-c_d/b})+o(1)\right\}.$$ The unit term in <a href="#con:density-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:density-budget">[con:density-budget]</a> is what leaves a positive mass after this subtraction. It remains to remove the composites whose two prime factors are both close to $\sqrt x$.

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
#### Composites with two nearly equal prime factors

<a id="con:boxes"></a>
A composite counted by $U_\kappa$ has exactly two prime factors with multiplicity: three factors greater than $x^{1/2-\kappa}$ would have product exceeding $2x$. Each factor lies in $[x^{1/2-\kappa},2x^{1/2+\kappa}]$. Cover the ordered factor pairs by dyadic boxes $$\label{con:boxes}
 \mathcal B=[M_1,2M_1)\times[M_2,2M_2)$$ that meet this range in each coordinate and admit a product in $[x,2x]$. They satisfy $x/4\le M_1M_2\le2x$, and their full coordinate intervals lie in $[x^{0.46},x^{0.54}]$ for large $x$. There are at most $C_{\rm dyad}(\kappa L+1)$ boxes, where $C_{\rm dyad}$ is absolute: this bounds the number of first coordinate dyads, and the product condition leaves only a bounded number of second coordinate dyads for each.

The rough-pair bound holds on a slightly wider fixed range of boxes, which includes all the boxes just selected.

<a id="stmt-con-rough-pairs"></a>
<div id="con:rough-pairs" class="lemma">

<a id="con:rough-box-range"></a>
<a id="con:rough-pair-bound"></a>
**Lemma 38** (An upper bound for rough pairs). *Let $\mathcal B=[M_1,2M_1)\times[M_2,2M_2)$ be any dyadic box such that $$\label{con:rough-box-range}
 x/4\le M_1M_2\le2x,\qquad
 x^{0.46}/2\le M_i\le2x^{0.54}\quad(i=1,2).$$ Then $$\label{con:rough-pair-bound}
 \sum_{\substack{(m,n)\in\mathcal B\\P^-(m)>W,\ P^-(n)>W}}w(mn)
       \le C_1X_0V(W)^2,$$ where $C_1$ depends at most on $M,\Psi$, and is independent of $K,\kappa,b,\varepsilon$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-con-rough-pairs"><summary>Proof of stmt-con-rough-pairs</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a contributing group integer $r\le x^{0.11}$, and set $k_r=cr$, $z'=x^{0.002}$. The corresponding pairs satisfy $mn\equiv1\pmod{k_r}$. At every odd prime $j\le z'$ with $j\nmid r$, they avoid <a id="con:pair-bad"></a>$$\label{con:pair-bad}
 mn\equiv1\pmod j,
 \qquad\text{and, if }j\le W,\qquad mn\equiv0\pmod j.$$ Indeed, $mn-1=k_rQ$ with $Q>x^{0.9}>z'$, while both factors are $W$-rough. We impose no further condition at primes dividing $k_r$ and may omit the fixed progression for this upper bound.

<a id="con:lattice-error"></a>
For a squarefree product $\ell$ of the designated odd primes, put $$\rho(\ell)=\prod_{j\mid\ell}
               \bigl(j-1+(2j-1)\mathbf{1}_{\{j\le W\}}\bigr).$$ The conditions $mn=1$ and $mn=0$ modulo $j$ are disjoint and have respectively $j-1$ and $2j-1$ residue pairs. There are $\varphi(k_r)$ pairs with product one modulo $k_r$. The Chinese remainder theorem therefore gives exactly $\varphi(k_r)\rho(\ell)$ pairs modulo $k_r\ell$ satisfying the base congruence and all the forbidden conditions at primes dividing $\ell$. Their count in the box is $$X'g(\ell)+E(\ell),\qquad
 X'=M_1M_2\frac{\varphi(k_r)}{k_r^2},\qquad
 g(\ell)=\frac{\rho(\ell)}{\ell^2},$$ where $$\label{con:lattice-error}
 E(\ell)\ll\varphi(k_r)\rho(\ell)
             \left(\frac{M_1+M_2}{k_r\ell}+1\right).$$ This formula also holds at $\ell=1$.

<a id="con:lattice-sum"></a>
The local bounds $g(j)\le7/9$ and $g(j)\le3/j$ verify the gap and block-sum hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#lem:block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:block">32</a>, with absolute constants. Its upper bound with $H=2$ uses $\ell\le(z')^{10}=x^{0.02}$. For squarefree $\ell$, $$\rho(\ell)\le\prod_{j\mid\ell}3j\le\tau(\ell)^2\ell,$$ where $\tau$ is the divisor function. Since $\varphi(k_r)\le k_r$ and $k_r=cr\le4r$, <a href="#con:lattice-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:lattice-error">[con:lattice-error]</a> gives $$|E(\ell)|\ll\tau(\ell)^2(M_1+M_2+r\ell).$$ The needed divisor sum follows elementarily. Let $\tau_4(n)$ count ordered decompositions of $n$ into four positive factors. Prime by prime, $\tau(n)^2\le\tau_4(n)$; summing over the first three factors therefore yields, for $Y\ge2$, $$\sum_{n\le Y}\tau(n)^2
 \le Y\left(\sum_{a\le Y}\frac1a\right)^3
 \ll Y\bigl(\log(2Y)\bigr)^3.$$ Consequently, taking $Y=x^{0.02}$ and using $M_1+M_2\le4x^{0.54}$ from <a href="#con:rough-box-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:rough-box-range">[con:rough-box-range]</a>, we obtain $$\label{con:lattice-sum}
 \sum_{\ell\le x^{0.02}}|E(\ell)|
       \ll (x^{0.56}+rx^{0.04})L^3
       =o\bigl((x/r)L^{-10}\bigr)$$ uniformly for $r\le x^{0.11}$. The ratios of the two displayed power terms to $x/r$ are at most $x^{-0.33}$ and $x^{-0.74}$, respectively, and absorb the logarithmic factor.

For $j\le W$, the surviving sieve factor is $$1-\frac{3j-2}{j^2}
       =\left(1-\frac1j\right)^3
                         \left(1-\frac1{(j-1)^2}\right),$$ and for $W<j\le z'$ it is $$1-\frac{j-1}{j^2}
       =\left(1-\frac1j\right)
                         \left(1+\frac1{j(j-1)}\right).$$ The correction products are bounded. Omitting the primes dividing $r$ costs a bounded factor by <a href="#con:omitted-primes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:omitted-primes">[con:omitted-primes]</a>, and omitting two costs a fixed factor. Thus $$\prod_{\substack{2<j\le z'\\j\nmid r}}(1-g(j))
       \ll V(W)^2V(z').$$ As $X'\ll x/r$, the upper sieve and <a href="#con:lattice-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:lattice-sum">[con:lattice-sum]</a> bound the pair count at this $r$ by $O((x/r)V(W)^2V(z'))$, uniformly in all the marks. Bound $\Psi$ by one, multiply by $\mathcal W(r)$, and sum. The result is $$O\bigl(xJ_0V(W)^2V(z')\bigr).$$ Finally $V(z')\asymp L^{-1}$, with fixed comparison constants, and <a href="#con:total-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:total-mass">[con:total-mass]</a> proves <a href="#con:rough-pair-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:rough-pair-bound">[con:rough-pair-bound]</a>. Only $J_0$, rather than the pointwise bound $B_K$, entered this calculation, so $C_1$ has the claimed independence. ◻

</details>

Return now to the boxes selected in <a href="#con:boxes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:boxes">[con:boxes]</a>, whose full coordinate intervals lie in $[x^{0.46},x^{0.54}]$. We replace the prime indicators in each of these boxes. Apply Lemma <a href="#con:type-ii" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="con:type-ii">34</a> with $$\gamma=0.4,\qquad s_-=0.46,\qquad s_+=0.54,
 \qquad\delta=b/2.$$ In this range $R_{0.4}(m)$ is exactly the prime indicator, since $2(0.4)>0.54$, and $D_{0.4}(s)=1/s$. Its comparison coefficient is therefore $$B_{\rm pr}(m)=\frac{\mathbf{1}_{\{P^-(m)>W\}}}{V(W)\log m}.$$ The identity $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbf{1}_{\{m\text{ prime}\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{n\text{ prime}\}}
       -B_{\rm pr}(m)B_{\rm pr}(n)
 &=(\mathbf{1}_{\{m\text{ prime}\}}-B_{\rm pr}(m))\mathbf{1}_{\{n\text{ prime}\}}\\
 &\quad+B_{\rm pr}(m)(\mathbf{1}_{\{n\text{ prime}\}}-B_{\rm pr}(n))
 \end{aligned}$$ allows two successive applications, interchanging the variables in the second. The companion coefficient is $W$-rough and bounded by one for large $x$ in both cases. All coefficients retain their box restrictions. The total error over the boxes is $O(xL^{-5})=o(X_0/L)$.

<a id="con:balanced-cost"></a>
Since $\log m,\log n\ge0.46L$, Lemma <a href="#con:rough-pairs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="con:rough-pairs">38</a> bounds the resulting sum in one box by $(0.46)^{-2}C_1X_0/L^2$. The ordered prime-pair sum includes prime squares and may count other semiprimes twice, which is harmless for an upper bound. Summing the boxes gives $$\label{con:balanced-cost}
 \sum_{\substack{P^-(d)>x^{1/2-\kappa}\\d\text{ composite}}}w(d)
       \le C_2(\kappa+L^{-1})\frac{X_0}{L}+o(X_0/L),$$ where $C_2=(0.46)^{-2}C_1C_{\rm dyad}$ depends at most on $M,\Psi$, and is independent of $K,\kappa,b,\varepsilon$.

<a id="pm-h-41"></a>
#### Choice of parameters and completion

<details id="proof-stmt-con-rough-pairs-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-con-rough-pairs</summary>

*Completion of the proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:controlled" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:controlled">[prop:controlled]</a>.* Fix $M,c,u,\Psi$ first, and hence the constants $\mathfrak S_M$ and $C_2$. Choose $0<\kappa<0.01$ such that $$C_2\kappa<\mathfrak S_M/4,$$ and then choose $0<\varepsilon<\kappa$. Choose $b>0$ sufficiently small for the sieve and density estimates, and so that $$\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma_E}}b
          (C_s\mathrm{e}^{-c_s/b}+C_d\mathrm{e}^{-c_d/b})<0.2.$$ Fix the partition used in the least-factor subtraction. Only finitely many Type II parameter sets now occur. Choose $K$ sufficiently large for all of them, and choose the exponents afterward, for example $$a_i=0.1+\frac{0.1i}{K+1}\qquad(1\le i\le K).$$ This order is permitted by Lemma <a href="#lem:marked-coefficient-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:marked-coefficient-transfer">30</a> and Proposition <a href="#prop:rough-type-ii" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:rough-type-ii">31</a>, hence by Lemma <a href="#con:type-ii" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="con:type-ii">34</a>.

Every supported prime is counted by $U_\kappa$. Subtracting <a href="#con:balanced-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:balanced-cost">[con:balanced-cost]</a> from <a href="#con:post-bin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:post-bin">[con:post-bin]</a>, and then taking $x$ sufficiently large for the fixed parameters, gives $$\frac{L}{\mathfrak S_M X_0}\sum_{d\text{ prime}}w(d)
       \ge0.9-0.2-\frac14+o(1)>\frac14.$$ By <a href="#con:total-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:total-mass">[con:total-mass]</a>, $J_0\ge1$, and $w\le B_K$, the number of distinct supported primes is therefore $\gg_{M,c,u}x/L^2$. Their support in <a href="#con:weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:weight">[con:weight]</a> ensures $x<d<2x$, $d\equiv u\pmod M$, and $d=crQ+1$ with $Q>x^{0.9}$ prime. Every prime factor of $r$ belongs to a group in <a href="#eq:marked-prime-groups" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marked-prime-groups">[eq:marked-prime-groups]</a>, hence lies in $(\exp(L^{0.1}),\exp(L^{0.3}))$ for all sufficiently large $x$. These are precisely the assertions of the proposition. ◻

</details>

<a id="app:whittaker"></a>
### The cubic theta normalization

This appendix supplies the Kazhdan–Patterson specialization and normalization behind [prop:theta-input](#prop:theta-input). We first specialize their cover, inducing datum, and global representation, and derive the normalized Whittaker factorization. We then verify the exact lift identities and compute the spherical Jacquet integral in the sections used in the paper. The local calculation fixes the Gauss phase and the unit factor together, and includes the modular factor in the triple-valuation step. The main argument uses the resulting global Fourier expansion with normalized local factors and the exact local rules for the Weyl calculation.

<a id="subsec:theta-specialization"></a>
#### The selected cover and global representation

<a id="eq:kp-torus-cocycle"></a>
Denote the cubic Hilbert symbol at a place $v$ by $(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)_{3,v}$. In the convention of Kazhdan and Patterson, the restriction of their determinant-modified cocycle to the diagonal torus is $$\label{eq:kp-torus-cocycle}
 \sigma_{c,v}\bigl(\operatorname{diag}(a,b),
                    \operatorname{diag}(a',b')\bigr)
 =(a,b')_{3,v}(ab,a'b')_{3,v}^{c},
 \qquad c\in\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}.$$ The root groups have their canonical splittings. These conventions are those of <a id="pm-cite-67"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Section 0.1, pp. 39–44].

For the $n$-fold cover of $\mathrm{GL}_d$, where $d$ is the rank parameter, the center criterion of <a id="pm-cite-68"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Proposition 0.1.1, p. 42] requires $z^{d-1+2dc}\in F_v^{\times n}$ for a scalar $zI$. For $d=2,n=3$ the exponent is $1+4c$, which is zero modulo three exactly when $c=2$. Moreover, <a href="#eq:kp-torus-cocycle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kp-torus-cocycle">[eq:kp-torus-cocycle]</a> on two scalars is $$\sigma_{2,v}(zI,z'I)=(z,z')_{3,v}^{\,1+4\cdot2}=1.$$ Thus their full preimage is central and the ordinary scalar section splits. Quotienting its image gives the asserted local covers of $\mathrm{PGL}_2$. The adelic cover and its rational splitting are obtained by the reciprocity construction in <a id="pm-cite-69"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Section 0.2]. The ordinary scalar section agrees with that splitting on rational scalars, so the quotient retains a rational splitting.

We check the datum in <a href="#eq:theta-central-datum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-central-datum">[eq:theta-central-datum]</a>. A Hilbert symbol with a cube argument is one; the cocycle on scalar pairs is also one. Consequently the cube and scalar sections commute and multiply without any remaining cocycle. If an element has two presentations in <a href="#eq:theta-central-datum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-central-datum">[eq:theta-central-datum]</a>, their two cube coordinates differ by a common scalar cube, so the displayed absolute-value ratio agrees. The formula is therefore a genuine character, trivial on the split scalar subgroup. On a coroot cube it gives $$\omega_v\bigl(s_v(\operatorname{diag}(t^3,t^{-3}))\bigr)=|t|_v.$$ This is exactly the exceptional condition of <a id="pm-cite-70"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Section I.2, p. 71]. The exceptional condition alone would only specify the square of the value on $a(\varpi^3)$; the datum in the main text chooses the pure absolute-value value.

Use the idele norm in <a href="#eq:theta-central-datum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-central-datum">[eq:theta-central-datum]</a> to define the global character. A rational diagonal lying in the adelic cube torus times scalars has a rational ratio which is a cube at every completion. If that ratio were not a cube in $F$, its nontrivial Kummer extension would have a nonsplit unramified prime by Chebotarev. Thus the ratio is a rational cube. The product formula now shows that our character is trivial on the intersection with the rational lift. This is the compatibility hypothesis in <a id="pm-cite-71"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Section II.1, p. 107, immediately after Lemma II.1.1]. Extending by the trivial character on rational lifts gives the character on the rational maximal abelian subgroup of that lemma. As in <a id="pm-cite-72"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Section II.1, pp. 107–109], one chooses an extension on the product of local maximal abelian subgroups which agrees with the former character on their intersection. The character on the scalar center is unitary.

At finite places, the local quotient and subrepresentation assertions are <a id="pm-cite-73"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Theorem I.2.9(a),(b), p. 72]. At tame finite places, part (f) of that theorem supplies the spherical vector for the normalized unramified datum. For a tame place, the uniqueness criterion is $$d=n-1,\quad 2(c+1)=0\pmod n,
 \qquad\text{or}\qquad d=n$$ by <a id="pm-cite-74"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Corollary I.3.6, p. 79]. It applies to $(d,n,c)=(2,3,2)$. The residue construction and restricted tensor product are <a id="pm-cite-75"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Theorem II.2.1, p. 118]. Since the tame local Whittaker spaces have dimension one, <a id="pm-cite-76"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Theorem II.2.5(a), p. 122] gives a nonzero global Whittaker functional, and part (b) gives local uniqueness at every place. At the complex places this also follows directly from <a id="pm-cite-77"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Theorem I.6.5(c), p. 105]. The inducing spaces, their exceptional images, and the automorphic residue all inherit the scalar action of $\omega$. That action is trivial, so these representations descend to the scalar quotients.

On the quotient torus the section of $a(y)$ has cocycle $(x,y)_{3,v}^{2}$. Since $-1$ is a cube in $F$, this is an alternating bimultiplicative law. At a tame place it is trivial on pairs of units, and its value on a uniformizer and a unit is the cubic residue symbol or its inverse, with a common orientation at all places. Choose the embedding $\iota$ with the orientation giving <a href="#eq:theta-effective-cocycle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-effective-cocycle">[eq:theta-effective-cocycle]</a>. The canonical compact splitting agrees with the sections of unit diagonals, Weyl elements, and integral upper root elements by <a id="pm-cite-78"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Proposition 0.1.3, p. 44]. Conjugating by the Weyl element gives the same agreement for the lower root group. <a id="pm-cite-79"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Corollary I.3.4, p. 78] gives relations for the coefficient functions defining local Whittaker functionals. <a id="pm-cite-80"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Theorems I.4.2–I.4.3, p. 87] convert them to spherical Whittaker values with the modular factor and give the central step. The direct local calculation below proves <a href="#eq:theta-local-values" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-local-values">[eq:theta-local-values]</a> and the lift identities <a href="#eq:theta-lift-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-lift-identities">[eq:theta-lift-identities]</a> in these conventions.

At a complex place the Hilbert symbol and the cover are trivial. <a id="pm-cite-81"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Theorem I.6.4(c), p. 104] identifies the exceptional representation with the ordinary principal series when $d\le n$. Our cube datum fixes the characters on all of $\mathbb{C}^\times$, because every complex number has a cube root. Triviality on scalars removes the determinant character; equivalently any possible remaining finite-order continuous character of the connected group $\mathbb{C}^\times$ is trivial. This gives the characters in part <span class="upright">(iii)</span> of [prop:theta-input](#prop:theta-input). Their Bessel function is computed in the main text from the Jacquet integral. Finally, the two Bruhat terms in the constant term of the Eisenstein series are described by <a id="pm-cite-82"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Proposition II.1.2, p. 110]. At the exceptional residue the surviving term belongs to the Weyl-conjugate induction, as in <a id="pm-cite-83"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Section II.1, p. 114]. Its complex inducing character, including the modular factor, is $|y|_v^{1/3}$; in particular it has no angular part.

<a id="subsec:theta-global-normalization"></a>
#### The normalized global Whittaker expansion

Let $\lambda$ be the nonzero global functional just obtained. The restricted tensor product is spanned by pure tensors, so there is a pure tensor $\varphi^\circ$ with $\lambda(\varphi^\circ)\ne0$. Its nondistinguished finite places form a finite set. The final $S$ contains this set; the arithmetic data are constructed only after that final choice. For $v\notin S$, varying only the $v$-component of $\varphi^\circ$ gives a nonzero local Whittaker functional. Normalize it at $\varphi_v^\circ$. If other finitely many components have already been varied, the resulting partial functional at $v$ is still a Whittaker functional, and local uniqueness makes it a scalar multiple of the normalized one. Evaluating at $\varphi_v^\circ$ identifies that scalar. Induction on the number of varied components proves <a href="#eq:theta-normalized-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-normalized-factorization">[eq:theta-normalized-factorization]</a>. The same argument factors the complex components.

At a finite product of remaining places the functional descends to the tensor product of the local twisted Jacquet quotients. The twisted Jacquet quotient of the principal series is finite dimensional by <a id="pm-cite-84"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Lemma I.3.2, p. 75]. Since $\Theta_v$ is its quotient by Theorem I.2.9(b), its twisted Jacquet quotient is a quotient of that finite-dimensional space. A functional on the finite tensor product is therefore a finite sum of products. This is the finite-S assertion in [prop:theta-input](#prop:theta-input).

This normalization accounts for the correction to the original global formula. The erratum withdraws an auxiliary spherical-functional vanishing assertion on pp. 119–120. It replaces stabilization of the auxiliary coefficient $C_S$ by stabilization of $C_ST(S)$, where in rank two $$T(S)=\prod_{\substack{v\in S\\v\nmid\infty}}(1+Q_v^{-1}),
 \qquad Q_v=\#(\mathcal O_v/\mathfrak p_v).$$ The sums in Theorems II.2.2–II.2.3 become finite-$S$ limits with the factor $T(S)^{-1}$, and the same change applies on p. 130 . The preceding argument begins with the nonzero automorphic functional and normalizes each exterior local functional at the distinguished spherical vector. It proves the product <a href="#eq:theta-normalized-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-normalized-factorization">[eq:theta-normalized-factorization]</a> without the withdrawn assertion or an unnormalized infinite product.

The rational splitting identifies every nonconstant Fourier coefficient with this Whittaker function. Conjugate $n(x)$ by the rational lift of $a(\nu)$ and change $x$ to $\nu x$; the adelic Jacobian is one. This gives <a href="#eq:theta-fourier-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-fourier-expansion">[eq:theta-fourier-expansion]</a>. On a fixed compact set of $g$, smoothness at infinity and invariance under a fixed finite open subgroup reduce the expansion along $F\backslash\mathbb{A}_F$ to the smooth Fourier expansion on a compact real torus. Integration by parts in sufficiently many archimedean directions gives absolute local uniform convergence there.

<a id="pm-h-45"></a>
#### Sections and the spherical induced vector

<a id="eq:app-torus-laws"></a>
Fix an exterior finite place $\mathfrak p$. In this appendix write $\mathcal O=\mathcal O_{\mathfrak p}$, let $\varpi$ be the chosen uniformizer, put $Q=q_{\mathfrak p}$, and abbreviate $\psi=\psi_{\mathfrak p}$, $\chi=\chi_{\mathfrak p}$, and $\mathcal C=\mathcal C_{\mathfrak p}$. Thus $\psi$ has conductor $\mathcal O$, additive measure gives $\mathcal O$ volume one, and $\chi$ is the sextic residue character on $\mathcal O^\times$. In all finite sums it is extended by zero to the zero residue, including when its exponent is zero. The embedding of the cubic symbol has been chosen so that the effective torus cocycle is $$\label{eq:app-torus-laws}
 \mathcal C(\varpi,u)=\chi(u)^{-2},\quad
 \mathcal C(u,\varpi)=\chi(u)^2,\quad
 \mathcal C(u,u')=1,\quad \mathcal C(\varpi,\varpi)=1
 \qquad(u,u'\in\mathcal O^\times).$$ The last two identities follow from tameness and alternation. In particular pure uniformizer powers multiply without a cocycle. All symbols with $-1$ are one because $-1\in F^{\times6}$.

<a id="eq:app-bruhat-lift"></a>
We first record explicitly why the ordinary rank-one decompositions are valid for these lifts. See Kubota <a id="pm-cite-85"></a>[[Kubota67](#ref-Kubota67), p. 114] for the local rank-one construction. In the convention of <a id="pm-cite-86"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Section 0.1, p. 41], the determinant-modified formula is $$\sigma_c(g_1,g_2)=
 \left(\frac{x(g_1g_2)}{x(g_1)},
       \frac{x(g_1g_2)}{x(g_2)\det g_1}\right)_3
       (\det g_1,\det g_2)_3^c,
 \quad
 x(g)=
 \begin{cases}
  g_{21},&g_{21}\ne0,\\
  g_{22},&g_{21}=0.
 \end{cases}$$ We take $c=2$ and then quotient the split scalar subgroup. Substitution in this formula gives $$n(x)a(y)=a(y)n(x/y),\qquad
 wa(y)=a(y^{-1})w$$ on the quotient. Before quotienting, the second equality has the extra scalar $yI$. All its cocycles are symbols between powers of $y$ and $-1$, hence are one. For $H\ne0$, the matrix identity $$wn(H)=n(H^{-1})\operatorname{diag}(-H^{-1},H)\bar n(H^{-1})$$ also lifts exactly. The product of the first two factors has cocycle one, and multiplication by the last factor has cocycle $(H^{-1},-H)_3=1$. The middle diagonal is the product of the split scalar $HI$ and $a(-H^{-2})$, again without a cocycle. Thus $$\label{eq:app-bruhat-lift}
 wn(H)=n(H^{-1})a(-H^{-2})\bar n(H^{-1})$$ on the quotient. If $H^{-1}\in\mathcal O$, the last factor is in the compact splitting. The latter agrees with the sections on unit diagonals, $w$, and integral upper root elements by <a id="pm-cite-87"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Proposition 0.1.3, p. 44]; conjugation by $w$ gives agreement on the lower root group. The diagonal $a(-1)$ can therefore be absorbed in a compact factor in any spherical calculation.

Let $\sigma^-$ be the unramified representation of the covering torus with central character the Weyl conjugate of <a href="#eq:theta-central-datum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-central-datum">[eq:theta-central-datum]</a>. Its norm exponent on $a(y)$ is $-1/6$. Modulo scalars, the subgroup of torus elements of valuation divisible by three is maximal abelian: the tame group $F_{\mathfrak p}^\times/F_{\mathfrak p}^{\times3}$ has one valuation and one unit coordinate over $\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}$, and the commutator pairing pairs those coordinates nondegenerately. The torus construction in <a id="pm-cite-88"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Section 0.3, pp. 54–55, and Section I.1, p. 58] realizes $\sigma^-$ by induction from a character of this subgroup. Its index is three, so the unramified Heisenberg representation has dimension three. The canonical unramified extension is trivial on unit lifts <a id="pm-cite-89"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Section I.1, p. 60]; the induced basis vector supported on that subgroup is therefore unit fixed. Call it $e_0$, and set <a id="eq:app-Heisenberg-basis"></a>$$\label{eq:app-Heisenberg-basis}
 e_j=Q^{-j/6}\sigma^-(a(\varpi^j))e_0,\qquad j\in\mathbb{Z}.$$ The central element $a(\varpi^3)$ acts by $Q^{1/2}$, so $e_{j+3}=e_j$. These three vectors form a basis. From <a href="#eq:app-torus-laws" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:app-torus-laws">[eq:app-torus-laws]</a> and unit invariance one obtains <a id="eq:app-torus-on-e0"></a>$$\label{eq:app-torus-on-e0}
 \sigma^-(a(\varpi^j u))e_0
   =Q^{j/6}\chi(u)^{2j}e_j
       \qquad(j\in\mathbb{Z},\ u\in\mathcal O^\times).$$

Use the vector-valued realization of normalized induction $$I^-=\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde B}^{\widetilde{\mathrm{PGL}}_2}
                   (\sigma^-),\qquad
 f(n(x)a(y)g)=|y|_{\mathfrak p}^{1/2}
                 \sigma^-(a(y))f(g).$$ Its spherical vector $f^\circ$ is determined by $f^\circ(k)=e_0$ on the compact subgroup. The total norm exponent in this transformation law is $1/2-1/6=1/3$. The compact-invariant line of this normalized unramified induction is one dimensional by <a id="pm-cite-90"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Lemma I.1.3, p. 60]. By <a id="pm-cite-91"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Theorem I.2.9(a),(f), p. 72], the local exceptional representation is the irreducible subrepresentation of $I^-$ and has a nonzero compact-invariant vector. That vector is a scalar multiple of $f^\circ$, so the subrepresentation contains $f^\circ$.

<a id="pm-h-46"></a>
#### The shells of the Jacquet integral

For a smooth $f\in I^-$, define the vector Jacquet functional by $$\mathscr J(f)=\lim_{L\to\infty}
   \int_{\varpi^{-L}\mathcal O}f(wn(x))\overline{\psi(x)}\,\,\mathrm{d}x.$$ This limit stabilizes. Indeed, when $|x|$ is sufficiently large, the right invariance of $f$ makes $\bar n(x^{-1})$ fix $f$. The lifted Bruhat formula then expresses $f(wn(x))$ as $$|x|_{\mathfrak p}^{-1}
       \sigma^-(a(-x^{-2}))f(1).$$ Choose $m$ so that $1+\varpi^m\mathcal O$ consists of cubes and its unit lifts fix the vector $f(1)$ in the smooth torus representation. On a shell $x=\varpi^{-l}v$, the displayed expression is unchanged when $v$ is replaced by $vu$, $u\in1+\varpi^m\mathcal O$: the norm is unchanged, all cocycles with $u$ are trivial, and $a(u^{-2})$ fixes $f(1)$. Each such unit coset is an additive coset of $\varpi^m\mathcal O$. For $l>m$, the character $\psi(\varpi^{-l}v)$ has zero integral on it. All sufficiently large shells therefore vanish. This argument applies to every smooth $f$, including each of its right translates.

If $t\in F_{\mathfrak p}$, choose a ball large enough to contain $t$ and to realize the stable values for both $f$ and $\pi(n(t))f$. Translation $x\mapsto x+t$ preserves that ball and gives $\mathscr J(\pi(n(t))f)=\psi(t)\mathscr J(f)$. Thus each scalar coordinate of $\mathscr J$ is a Whittaker functional. This is a direct stable version, at the present inducing parameter, of the Jacquet functionals introduced in <a id="pm-cite-92"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Section I.3, pp. 74–75].

<a id="eq:app-Jacquet"></a>
For $e\ge0$ put $J_e=\mathscr J(\pi(a(\varpi^e))f^\circ)$; explicitly, $$\label{eq:app-Jacquet}
 J_e=\int_{F_{\mathfrak p}}
        f^\circ(wn(x)a(\varpi^e))\overline{\psi(x)}\,\,\mathrm{d}x.$$ The integral retains the stable interpretation just established. We now evaluate it shell by shell.

<a id="eq:app-common-factor"></a>
Put $x=\varpi^eH$ and use the first two lift identities above. The measure contributes $Q^{-e}$, the modular factor of $a(\varpi^{-e})$ contributes $Q^{e/2}$, and its torus norm factor contributes $Q^{-e/6}$. Thus every shell has the common factor $$\label{eq:app-common-factor}
 Q^{-e+e/2-e/6}=Q^{-2e/3}.$$ If $H\in\mathcal O$, then $wn(H)$ belongs to the compact subgroup, and $\psi(\varpi^eH)=1$. This region contributes $e_{-e}$ inside the common factor.

<a id="eq:app-shell"></a>
Suppose instead that $H=\varpi^{-l}v$, with $l\ge1$ and $v\in\mathcal O^\times$. In <a href="#eq:app-bruhat-lift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:app-bruhat-lift">[eq:app-bruhat-lift]</a> the last factor is compact, while the first acts trivially in the inducing transformation law. The modular factor of $a(-H^{-2})$ is $Q^{-l}$, and its torus norm factor is $Q^{l/3}$. Furthermore, $$a(\varpi^{2l}v^{-2})
   =\mathcal C(\varpi^{2l},v^{-2})^{-1}
       a(\varpi^{2l})a(v^{-2}),\qquad
 \mathcal C(\varpi^{2l},v^{-2})^{-1}=\chi(v)^{-8l}.$$ The minus sign in $-H^{-2}$ has no effect. Multiplication by $a(\varpi^{-e})$ changes the basis index by $-e$, without a cocycle between the pure uniformizer powers. Finally $\,\mathrm{d}H=Q^l\,\,\mathrm{d}v$. It follows that this shell contributes, inside <a href="#eq:app-common-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:app-common-factor">[eq:app-common-factor]</a>, $$\label{eq:app-shell}
 Q^{l/3}e_{-e+2l}
       \int_{\mathcal O^\times}
         \chi(v)^{-8l}\overline{\psi(\varpi^{e-l}v)}\,\,\mathrm{d}v.$$ This accounts separately for the measure, modular, norm, and Hilbert-symbol factors.

Define the normalized prime Gauss sums $$g_j=Q^{-1/2}\sum_{x\bmod\mathfrak p}
          \chi(x)^j\psi(x/\varpi),\qquad j\in\mathbb{Z}.$$ The powers in these sums retain the unit mask. Since $\chi(-1)=1$, replacing $\psi(x/\varpi)$ by its complex conjugate has no effect on $g_j$. For $j\not\equiv0\pmod6$, the usual finite-field Gauss identity gives $$|g_j|=1,\qquad g_jg_{-j}=\chi(-1)^j=1.$$ In particular $g_2g_4=1$. These identities follow, for example, by expanding the product of the two sums, substituting $x=ty$, and using the additive sum in $y$; the result is $Q\chi(-1)^j$ before division by $Q$.

Write $I_{e,l}$ for the integral in <a href="#eq:app-shell" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:app-shell">[eq:app-shell]</a>. The character there is $\chi^{-2l}$, since $-8l\equiv-2l\pmod6$. Its exact values are <a id="eq:app-shell-integrals"></a>$$\label{eq:app-shell-integrals}
 I_{e,l}=
 \begin{cases}
  1-Q^{-1},&1\le l\le e,\quad 3\mid l,\\
  0,&1\le l\le e,\quad 3\nmid l,\\
  Q^{-1/2}g_{-2l},&l=e+1,\quad 3\nmid l,\\
  -Q^{-1},&l=e+1,\quad 3\mid l,\\
  0,&l>e+1.
 \end{cases}$$ Indeed, for $l\le e$ the additive character is trivial, so unit orthogonality gives the first two cases. For $l=e+1$, integration over the residue classes gives $Q^{-1}$ times the corresponding prime Gauss sum; the trivial character with its unit mask gives $\sum_{x\ne0}\psi(x/\varpi)=-1$. For $l>e+1$, split each unit class modulo $\varpi^{l-e-1}$ into its $Q$ lifts modulo $\varpi^{l-e}$. The multiplicative character is unchanged on these lifts, whereas the additive factors sum to $\sum_{t\bmod\mathfrak p}\psi(t/\varpi)=0$. This also proves the asserted stabilization of <a href="#eq:app-Jacquet" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:app-Jacquet">[eq:app-Jacquet]</a>.

<a id="eq:app-J-values"></a>
We can now sum the shells. For $k\ge0$, the integral unit ball and the shells $l=3,6,\ldots,3k$ have the same basis index and combined coefficient $$1+(1-Q^{-1})\sum_{j=1}^{k}Q^j=Q^k.$$ For $e=3k$, the terminal shell $l=3k+1$ has coefficient $Q^{k-1/6}g_4$ and basis vector $e_2$. For $e=3k+1$, the terminal shell $l=3k+2$ has coefficient $Q^{k+1/6}g_2$ and basis vector $e_0$. For $e=3k+2$, the terminal shell $l=3k+3$ has coefficient $-Q^k$ and the same basis vector $e_1$ as the preceding combined contribution. Multiplying by <a href="#eq:app-common-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:app-common-factor">[eq:app-common-factor]</a> therefore gives $$\begin{aligned}
 J_{3k}
   &=Q^{-k}(e_0+Q^{-1/6}g_4e_2),\notag\\
 J_{3k+1}
   &=Q^{-k}(Q^{-1/2}g_2e_0+Q^{-2/3}e_2)
     =Q^{-k-1/2}g_2(e_0+Q^{-1/6}g_4e_2),\notag\\
 J_{3k+2}&=0.                                      \label{eq:app-J-values}
\end{aligned}$$ The second equality uses $g_2g_4=1$.

<a id="eq:app-pure-values"></a>
In particular $J_0=e_0+Q^{-1/6}g_4e_2\ne0$. Choose a scalar coordinate nonzero on $J_0$. Its Jacquet functional is nonzero on the exceptional subrepresentation, since that subrepresentation contains $f^\circ$. The local Whittaker space is one dimensional by <a id="pm-cite-93"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Corollary I.3.6, p. 79]. Hence every spherical Whittaker function normalized at one has the ratios in <a href="#eq:app-J-values" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:app-J-values">[eq:app-J-values]</a>: $$\label{eq:app-pure-values}
 W(a(\varpi^{3k}))=Q^{-k},\qquad
 W(a(\varpi^{3k+1}))=Q^{-k-1/2}g_2,\qquad
 W(a(\varpi^{3k+2}))=0.$$ The middle term is $Q^{-k-1}\sum_x\chi(x)^2\psi(x/\varpi)$, as claimed. This computation agrees with the central-step statement of <a id="pm-cite-94"></a>[[KP84](#ref-KP84), Theorem I.4.3, p. 87]: on $a(\varpi^3)$ the Weyl-conjugate inducing character is $Q^{1/2}$ and the modular half-character is $Q^{-3/2}$, so their product is $Q^{-1}$.

<a id="pm-h-47"></a>
#### Negative valuations and the unit normalization

<a id="eq:app-unit-rule"></a>
For $e<0$, choose $x\in\mathcal O$ for which $\psi(\varpi^e x)\ne1$. The spherical vector is fixed by $n(x)$, while the split root relation and Whittaker equivariance give $$W(a(\varpi^e))
 =W(a(\varpi^e)n(x))
 =\psi(\varpi^e x)W(a(\varpi^e)).$$ Thus the value is zero. For a unit $u$, the compact section of $a(u)$ fixes the spherical vector. The torus law gives $$a(\varpi^e u)=\mathcal C(\varpi^e,u)^{-1}a(\varpi^e)a(u),$$ where $\mathcal C$ includes the scalar action on a genuine vector. Consequently $$\label{eq:app-unit-rule}
 W(a(\varpi^e u))=\chi(u)^{2e}W(a(\varpi^e)).$$ Together with <a href="#eq:app-pure-values" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:app-pure-values">[eq:app-pure-values]</a>, this proves <a href="#eq:theta-local-values" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-local-values">[eq:theta-local-values]</a>.

There is a useful check that fixes the orientation without referring to a convention for the name of a cubic Gauss sum. At a tame place the residue character is unchanged when $\varpi$ is replaced by $\varpi u$. Substitution $x=uv$ in the residue sum yields $$Q^{-1}\sum_{x\bmod\mathfrak p}\chi(x)^2\psi(x/(\varpi u))
 =\chi(u)^2 Q^{-1}\sum_{v\bmod\mathfrak p}
                         \chi(v)^2\psi(v/\varpi).$$ This is exactly the $e=1$ transformation in <a href="#eq:app-unit-rule" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:app-unit-rule">[eq:app-unit-rule]</a>. Thus the Gauss phase and the unit factor are a single compatible normalization. Changing the sign of an additive Gauss argument is harmless because $\chi(-1)=1$.
